a-perfect-treat-for-st-patrick-s-day.mdx:

```---
title: "A perfect treat for St. Patrick’s Day"
date: 2020-02-26
draft: true
description: "I’ve been active in the competitive Cake World for more than 10 years now, but there is one confection that just seems to bring more joy to people than anything else: the cookie. It makes …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/a-perfect-treat-for-st-patricks-day,35972"
image: "@assets/blog/a-perfect-treat-for-st-patrick-s-day/20200224-085047-St_Patricks_Owl_Cookie_finished_piece.jpg"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/a-perfect-treat-for-st-patrick-s-day/20200224-085047-St_Patricks_Owl_Cookie_finished_piece.jpg';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/a-perfect-treat-for-st-patrick-s-day/20200224-084938-Owl_20cookie_201.jpg';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/a-perfect-treat-for-st-patrick-s-day/20200224-084946-Owl_20cookie_202.jpg';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/a-perfect-treat-for-st-patrick-s-day/20200224-084954-Owl_20cookie_203.jpg';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/a-perfect-treat-for-st-patrick-s-day/20200224-085004-Owl_20cookie_204.jpg';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/a-perfect-treat-for-st-patrick-s-day/20200224-085013-Owl_20cookie_205.jpg';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/a-perfect-treat-for-st-patrick-s-day/20200224-085022-Owl_20cookie_206.jpg';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/a-perfect-treat-for-st-patrick-s-day/20200224-085037-Owl_20cookie_207.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

I’ve been active in the competitive Cake World for more than 10 years now, but there is one confection that just seems to bring more joy to people than anything else: the cookie.

It makes perfect sense if you think about it. How many people don’t like cookies? I’m pretty sure my ability to bake good cookies got me through high school math class, but that’s a story for another day.

When most of us were little, helping to decorate cookies was a special treat that brought us a unique kind of joy.

It still can.

During the third week of December 2019, I had the privilege of teaching cookie decoration techniques to a variety of kids. One evening, I worked with two groups of Daisies, thanks to local Girl Scout Troop Leaders Julie Buck and Julie Potosek. Daisies are girls aged 5 to 7, and I was amazed by how good their work was. All 20 of them left that night with a cookie to be proud of.

Then, later that week—thanks to the Sullivan County BOCES Arts in Education program and high school art teachers Kath Johanson and Jennifer Bull—I taught a series of cookie decorating classes to 120 older students, including members of the National Art Honor Society.

Once again, the students were really into it. The power of the cookie cannot be denied.

With this tutorial, I hope to help you create this impossibly cute, St. Patrick’s Day-themed owl cookie. It isn’t as tough as it might look. Feel free to experiment with the techniques set out here and try your own ideas. After all, part of the joy of cookie decoration is expressing yourself—just like kids have done in Grandma’s kitchen for years!

Watch the video or follow the step-by-step instructions below.

<Youtube id="aoQBbCNVOFc" title="YouTube video" client:load />

**Tools needed:**

5-inch round sugar cookie
white fondant
Royal Icing
food coloring
squirt bottles
piping bags
small round piping tips
toothpicks
small paintbrush
petal dust

Take your 5-inch round sugar cookie and cover it with a thin layer of white fondant.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Sketch out the owl design on the fondant using a pointy clay tool.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Outline your design with icing; it should be the consistency of a thick paste. Use a piping bag with a small tip to outline the design. If the icing is too thin, add some more confectioners sugar. You will need a steady hand.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

When you are finished piping, set the cookie aside to dry.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Fill in your solid color**s** using the squeeze bottles containing a thinner icing, using a toothpick to help spread it. The icing should be the consistency of gravy (this process is called "flooding”). Let dry a little before painting and dusting.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using a small brush, paint in eyes and details.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using a small, dry brush, dust in highlights and shadows.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>
```


create-this-precious-easter-basket-out-of-chocolate.mdx:

```---
title: "Create this precious Easter basket out of chocolate"
date: 2020-03-25
draft: true
description: "Around the first of the month, I saw eight robins hanging out in my backyard—always a welcome sign for me that spring isn’t far away. Yes, I admit that I feed birds year-round, having …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/create-this-precious-easter-basket-out-of-chocolate,36620"
image: "@assets/blog/create-this-precious-easter-basket-out-of-chocolate/20200325-094029-Easter_Basket_Out_of_Chocolate.JPG"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/create-this-precious-easter-basket-out-of-chocolate/20200325-094029-Easter_Basket_Out_of_Chocolate.JPG';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/create-this-precious-easter-basket-out-of-chocolate/20200325-091848-Easter_20Basket_20Out_20of_20Chocolate_202.JPG';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/create-this-precious-easter-basket-out-of-chocolate/20200325-091855-Easter_20Basket_20Out_20of_20Chocolate_203.JPG';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/create-this-precious-easter-basket-out-of-chocolate/20200325-091900-Easter_20Basket_20Out_20of_20Chocolate_204.jpg';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/create-this-precious-easter-basket-out-of-chocolate/20200325-091906-Easter_20Basket_20Out_20of_20Chocolate_205.JPG';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/create-this-precious-easter-basket-out-of-chocolate/20200325-091911-Easter_20Basket_20Out_20of_20Chocolate_206.JPG';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/create-this-precious-easter-basket-out-of-chocolate/20200325-091915-Easter_20Basket_20Out_20of_20Chocolate_207.JPG';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/create-this-precious-easter-basket-out-of-chocolate/20200325-091918-Easter_20Basket_20Out_20of_20Chocolate_208.JPG';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/create-this-precious-easter-basket-out-of-chocolate/20200325-091924-Easter_20Basket_20Out_20of_20Chocolate_209.jpg';
import img10 from '@assets/blog/create-this-precious-easter-basket-out-of-chocolate/20200325-091929-Easter_20Basket_20Out_20of_20Chocolate_2010.JPG';
import img11 from '@assets/blog/create-this-precious-easter-basket-out-of-chocolate/20200325-091937-Easter_20Basket_20Out_20of_20Chocolate_2011.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Around the first of the month, I saw eight robins hanging out in my backyard—always a welcome sign for me that spring isn’t far away.

Yes, I admit that I feed birds year-round, having finally discovered a squirrel-proof bird feeder that is actually squirrel proof. And since those little gray tormenters don’t even bother trying to get in anymore, there is no lack of feathered-friend sightings in my neck of the literal woods.

But the robins mean spring, and spring means hikes outside and the onset of warmer weather to come—always preferable for me, since I love being outside without freezing.

Spring also comes with traditions and holidays: Spring cleaning, Passover, Easter.

Of course, in the competitive Cake World, Easter is huge—the bunnies, eggs, candy and baskets associated with its more secular celebrations are ideal for cake and sugar artists to go wild... which we often do.

I thought, given the season, that it would be fun to show our readers how to make an Easter basket of their own out of chocolate.

I thought it might also be interesting to hear a little bit about the origin of the Easter basket. An article in Scientific American, “What’s the Meaning of Easter Baskets?” by Krystal D’Costa, explains the origin:

“Easter is ultimately a celebration of spring. This is a period traditionally where life returns to an otherwise stark and cold landscape, therefore it’s not surprising that the key representatives of this season—the egg and the rabbit—are so heavily steeped in fertility symbolism.... Baskets do have a role to play in that particular narrative. They’re symbolic nests, after all, and are specifically used by children in modern Easter egg hunts to carry their prizes. Filled to the brim with eggs and other treasures, they are the epitome of birth and potential.”

So, what could be better than creating an Easter Basket that can be eaten itself?

<Youtube id="vs4nRzIc5U4" title="YouTube video" client:load />

Materials:
modeling chocolate (I prefer Satin Ice Choco Pan)
rolling pin
paintbrushes
clay tools
petal punch
petal dust (I prefer The Sugar Art Colors)
small Styrofoam bowl
jellybeans
water
cornstarch
wafer paper (I prefer Icing Images)


Make about 18 1-inch balls out of the modeling chocolate

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>


Roll the balls thin, about 11 inches long and about 1/4” thick

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>


Lay three strips over the bottom of the bowl

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>


Use a simple weave pattern to form the basket

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>


Use a little water to cement the connections, then twist off the tips and sculpt the rim

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>


Use a clay tool to add texture and refrigerate for at least 15 minutes while you make some flowers

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>


Use the petal punch to make flowers

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>


Pull basket out of Styrofoam and paint

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img9} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>


Paint basket with brown petal dust

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img10} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>


Add flowers with a little water, then paint with petal dust.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img11} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Cut grass strips out of wafer paper and lay in basket.

Add candy, and you’re all set!
```


halloween-food-art.mdx:

```---
title: "Halloween food art"
date: 2024-10-23
description: "I absolutely love Halloween. One of the biggest pleasures for me is that you get to dress up and be whatever you want to be. Plus, I love the ghoulish imagery, the creepy lights and the elements of …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags:
  [
    halloween,
    food-art,
    halloween-wars,
    tv-competition,
    spooky-treats,
    diy-decorating,
    pumpkin-carving,
    holiday-decorating,
  ]
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/halloween-food-art,169365"
image: "@assets/blog/halloween-food-art/halloween-food-art.jpeg"
imageAlt: "An assortment of halloween-themed treats in glass covered with edible spiderwebs"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from "astro:assets";
import img1 from "@assets/blog/halloween-food-art/eddie-display.jpg";
import img2 from "@assets/blog/halloween-food-art/skeleton-chair.jpeg";
import img3 from "@assets/blog/halloween-food-art/ghost.jpeg";
import img4 from "@assets/blog/halloween-food-art/skeleton-car.jpeg";

I absolutely love Halloween. One of the biggest pleasures for me is that you get to dress up and be whatever you want to be. Plus, I love the ghoulish imagery, the creepy lights and the elements of magic that surround this time of year.

Some of the best memories I have as a kid involve trick-or-treating on Halloween night in my neighborhood in Liberty, NY. Just about every house was decorated in full Halloween splendor, and the massive numbers of costumed candy claimers were never turned away without a bag full of delicious confections.

Now, regular readers of Kim’s Kitchen know that I have been on a number of Food Network competition programs. I’m not looking to brag, but I have won a couple of them—“Cake Wars: Star Wars” and “Holiday Wars” among them.

But my favorite show? “Halloween Wars,” without question.

I was privileged to appear on Season 11, which aired during the fall of 2021. Season 11 was a major departure from the first 10 seasons of “Halloween Wars.” To begin with, long-time host Jonathan Bennett was replaced by noted paranormal investigator Zak Bagans (Jonathan has since returned). Then, too, the overall look of the set and feel of the challenges took a turn for the scarier.

And finally, because we shot Season 11 during the height of the COVID pandemic, pumpkins were out of season—so the pumpkin-carving aspect of “Halloween Wars” had to be eliminated.

And let me tell you, the hatred spewing forth on social media over that development was epic! Commentators were vowing to swear off “Halloween Wars” forever, completely oblivious to the fact that there wouldn’t have been a Season 11 at all if we’d waited for the End of the World to, well, end. (I feel like there are still elements of COVID that are crouching in the shadows, waiting to raise their ugly, panicky heads once more.)

That our team—“Ghosts With the Most” (Lily Halabi, Jewel Burgess and me)—made it into the finals was very special to me. And even though we lost, I’m very proud of what my teammates and I accomplished. Whenever viewers say to me, “You got robbed,” I count it as an artistic victory (and by that, I mean no disrespect to the Mischievous Monsters, who actually won—it was a close call, but they did undeniably great work as well).

Given all of this, you would think that there would be no one who loves Halloween more than I do.

And yet, there is.

Allow me to introduce you to Mr. Halloween, otherwise known in the mortal world as Eddie (I’m going to keep things mysterious by not providing his last name, but I assure you he is a real person—or is he?…)

When it comes to Halloween decorating and celebrating, Eddie and his heroic wife Nancy are second to none.

<figure class="blog-image">
  <Image
    src={img3}
    alt="A spooky ghost in the foreground of a house with Halloween decorations"
  />
  <figcaption>
    Eddie's decorations are spooky without being overly gory.
  </figcaption>
</figure>

No, I’m not going to tell you where Eddie and Nancy live—there are some people out there who are dangerous, after all. But I will tell you that you will know their house if you see it.

For Eddie, it all dates back to childhood.

“It’s something I grew up with since around the age of 10, or maybe even younger,” he’ll tell you with a smile. “I’m in my late 50s now, so I’ve known about Halloween for a long while. I’m the oldest of two brothers, and my parents always supported it, welcoming the neighborhood kids. I credit my brother for passing on the decorating bug to me. It’s a great creative mindset!”

<figure class="blog-image">
  <Image
    src={img1}
    alt="A wall inside a house decorated for Halloween with spooky props including skeletons, tombstones and orange lighting"
  />
  <figcaption>
    For Eddie, Halloween isn't just one day. It lives in his heart all year.
  </figcaption>
</figure>

Anybody who has ever visited Eddie’s “haunted mansion” at this time of year knows exactly how creative he can be.

“I’ve gone from displaying things haphazardly to setting specific scenes, without being over-the-top gory or bloody,” he explains.

<figure class="blog-image">
  <Image
    src={img4}
    alt="A skeleton decoration buckled up in the backseat of a car"
  />
  <figcaption>Eddie sets the scene on Halloween.</figcaption>
</figure>

For Eddie and Nancy, the decorating experience has been evolving for 24 years.

“We started in 2000, when we bought our first house,” Eddie says. “Since then, the number of inside and outside displays have changed and expanded. And so have the number of trick-or-treaters!”

Have any of the decorations remained constant throughout that time period?

“Some have remained for years, but I don’t have a blueprint for what goes where,” Eddie says. “Incorporating everyday items like spotlights, fencing, orange pylon cones, garden tools and caution tape have enhanced the look over the years as well.”

<figure class="blog-image">
  <Image
    src={img2}
    alt="A skeleton figure sitting in a rocking chair in front of a porch with fallen leaves around him for Halloween"
  />
  <figcaption>A skeleton greets visitors at Eddie's place.</figcaption>
</figure>

For Eddie, Halloween isn’t just one day. It lives in his heart all year.

“I count down the time to Halloween,” he laughs. “I can tell people on any given day how far away we are from October 31. I also tell people that it takes about two weeks to decorate my house, when in reality, it’s closer to two months. When folks ask me how I honor Halloween, my number-one answer is, ‘I decorate!’”

Of course, decorating isn’t all Eddie does.

“I have Halloween-themed, collared button-down shirts,” he says, “plus, we’ve hosted parties with costume prizes, put on shows, played seasonal music and hosted movies.”

When not engaged in Halloweening, Eddie hosts a popular podcast.

“It’s called “The Marvelists,” he says. “I co-host it with Peter Melnick. We began in 2018, primarily to review the films in the Marvel Comic Universe. We’ve expanded to include non-Marvel artists, writers and creators, as well as wrestlers, actors, convention promoters and fellow podcasters. I also do a one-on-one interview feature called Cosplay Connection with folks who attend shows in costume. And I do cosplay photography as a hobby.”

That’s why this month’s Kim’s Kitchen project is Halloween Food Art. These treats are delicious, simple to make, and honor Eddie’s do-it-yourself-style mantra when it comes to holiday decorating. Experiment with your favorite shapes, colors, and flavors—and let your creativity be your ghoulish guide.

Mr. Halloween would expect no less.

<Youtube
  id="xUxMzefVq5w"
  title="Halloween Food Art video tutorial"
  client:load
/>
```


halloween-lollipops.mdx:

```---
title: "Halloween lollipops"
date: 2022-10-25
draft: true
description: "Devin Michael Housman loved Halloween. As a child, Devin was diagnosed with autism. The Housman family—mother Yvonne, father Jim, and older sister Briana—faced many …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/halloween-lollipops,65905"
image: "@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151650-43fd-_Kim-_main.jpg"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151650-43fd-_Kim-_main.jpg';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151653-Devin.jpg';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151654-Devin_27s_20art.jpg';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151715-Halloween_20lolipops_20steps.jpg';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151655-Halloween_20Lolipops_201.jpg';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151657-Halloween_20Lolipops_202.jpg';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151701-Halloween_20Lolipops_203.jpg';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151702-Halloween_20Lolipops_204.jpg';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151704-Halloween_20Lolipops_205.jpg';
import img10 from '@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151705-Halloween_20Lolipops_206.jpg';
import img11 from '@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151707-Halloween_20Lolipops_207.jpg';
import img12 from '@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151708-Halloween_20Lolipops_208.jpg';
import img13 from '@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151710-Halloween_20Lolipops_209.jpg';
import img14 from '@assets/blog/halloween-lollipops/20221025-151712-Halloween_20Lolipops_2010.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        The completed Halloween lollipops.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

 Devin Michael Housman loved Halloween.

 As a child, Devin was diagnosed with autism. The Housman family—mother Yvonne, father Jim, and older sister Briana—faced many challenges because of it.

 But no one faced more challenges than Devin himself. Yet he loved life.

 “I know I’m different, Mommy,” he would say. “And that’s OK.”

 Devin loved drawing, the theater, drinking Coca-Cola, and watching movies—especially the original cartoon version of “The Lion King.” He wouldn’t sleep until he’d viewed the film on a well-worn VHS player.

 So, naturally, when Halloween came along, one of his go-to costumes was a lion. And he insisted on sleeping in his Halloween pajamas.

 As he got older, Devin decided that costumes weren’t for him anymore. But that didn’t stop his love for the rest of his Halloween traditions.

 “He always picked out the candy,” Jim Housman says. “Sometimes, he’d make us drive down to Gertrude Hawk Chocolates in Middletown [NY] so he could grab what he wanted.”

 To get into the spirit of the season, the Housmans always took Devin over to what was then Maples Farm in Middletown. There, he would run through the hay maze, feed the animals, and attempt to ring the gong on the High Striker.

 “And he always got the biggest pumpkin for his sister,” Yvonne Housman said. “He’d paint it rather than carve it.”

 At Devin’s school—Sullivan ARC—his penchant for organizing activities earned him a nickname: the Social Director. His goal in life was to work behind the scenes in a movie theater, because he believed that movies made people happy.

 Of course, his love of popcorn might have had something to do with it, too.

 In any case, on Halloween night, the Social Director made sure everything went off without a hitch—especially the distribution of the precious candy.

 “What do you say?” he’d sing to the costumed kiddies on his doorstep, waiting for a hearty “Trick or Treat” before gifting them with confections.

 Sadly, Devin’s life ended unexpectedly on June 6, 2020. He was only 22.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Devin Michael Housman
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        A sample of Devin's artwork. He loved Halloween.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

 In Devin’s honor, the Housmans have established The Devin Michael Housman Memorial Scholarship, which is awarded annually to one graduating student from a high school in Orange County, and one from Sullivan County. The students receiving the award have had special education classifications (IEPs), and will be seeking careers in the arts. The students will have also displayed kindness to others—as Devin did.

 A fundraiser for the scholarship will be held on Saturday, November 19 at El Sombrero Restaurant, 227 Lake Louise Marie Rd., Rock Hill, NY. The event runs from 12 noon to 4 p.m. Tickets are available, and cost $40 per person—which includes food—and can be purchased by calling 845/807-8131, or by emailing masten@hvc.rr.com.

 It’s a wonderful event to honor a wonderful person, and a chance to give back to the community.

 That’s why I chose these Halloween lollipops for this month’s project. I have an autistic grandson, so this cause is very close to me. And, like Devin, I love Halloween. Plus, these lollipops are lively, easy to make, and they really capture the fun side of the holiday—something I know Devin Michael Housman would have truly appreciated.

<Youtube id="NTxnBvy6Ags" title="Make These Delicious Halloween Lollipops" client:load />

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        You'll need Cello Sheets to make the lollipops.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Open edible Cello Sheets with pre-printed Halloween designs.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Remove the backing.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Cut out the images with scissors...
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img9} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Place images in silicone lollipop molds...
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img10} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Heat up clear isomalt with added candy flavoring in the microwave: 30 seconds initially, then in 15-second intervals until it bubbles. NOTE: Isomalt is HOT! Use caution and wear gloves.....
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img11} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Pour isomalt evenly into the molds and let cool.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img12} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Use a blowtorch to remove bubbles from lollipops. Let cool...
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img13} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Test with a cake tool to see if lollipops have hardened...Remove your lollipops from the molds...
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img14} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Dip the tip of the lollipop sticks into the warm isomalt and place on the back of your lollipops. Let cool...Use the blow torch to shine your lollipops one last time. Let cool, and enjoy!..
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Halloween Lollipop Steps

- Open edible Cello Sheets with pre-printed Halloween designs and remove backing.
- Cut out the images with scissors.
- Place images in silicone lollipop molds.
- Heat up clear isomalt with added candy flavoring in the microwave: 30 seconds initially, then in 15-second intervals until it bubbles. NOTE: Isomalt is HOT! Use caution and wear gloves.
- Pour isomalt evenly into the molds and let cool.
- Use a blow torch to remove bubbles from lollipops. Let cool.
- Test with a cake tool to see if lollipops have hardened.
- Remove your lollipops from the molds.
- Dip the tip of the lollipop sticks into the warm isomalt and place on the back of your lollipops. Let cool.
- Use the blow torch to shine your lollipops one last time. Let cool, and enjoy!
```


how-and-why-i-made-a-life-sized-deer-cake.mdx:

```---
title: "How and why I made a life-sized deer cake"
date: 2024-03-26
draft: true
description: "“Would you be interested in making a life-sized mounted deer cake for my hunting camp’s one-hundredth anniversary?” I’ve had many an offbeat conversation with my friend …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/how-and-why-i-made-a-life-sized-deer-cake,141017"
image: "@assets/blog/how-and-why-i-made-a-life-sized-deer-cake/20240326-094026-13fd-_Kim-_Kim_and_deer.jpg"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/how-and-why-i-made-a-life-sized-deer-cake/20240326-094026-13fd-_Kim-_Kim_and_deer.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Kim M. Simons poses with the life-sized deer cake she created for the Cherokee Preserve Club's 100th anniversary.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

“Would you be interested in making a life-sized mounted deer cake for my hunting camp’s one-hundredth anniversary?”

I’ve had many an offbeat conversation with my friend Glenn Horton. But none of them have started out like this.

Glenn’s an interesting guy. He’s recently retired from a productive career at the NYC Department of Environmental Protection. He’s the first person I ever met who pilots hot air balloons. Plus, he’s a nature lover and an active member of the informal Friends of Walnut Mountain group out of Liberty, NY.

I was hiking Walnut when I met Glenn and his long-time partner Sharon Zimmerman working on wheelchair-accessible trails. We hit it off right away.

Glenn and Sharon are supportive of me and my work, and are always asking me what I’m up to in the Cake World. So, when the Cherokee Preserve Club in Bethel, NY hit the century mark, Glenn wanted to commemorate the occasion with a one-of-a-kind, knock-out cake. And he wanted me to do it.

I’m not looking to brag, but I’ve done a number of big pieces in the past. I’ve sculpted wolves and giant turtles out of chocolate live in Busch Gardens, VA while passers-by stopped and watched. I sculpted a goofy Tyrannosaurus about the size of a basset hound—with a selfie stick—as well. And my teammate Chef Blāque Shelton and I sculpted a life-sized Lady Gaga on “Cakealikes,” which aired on Food Network on January 4, 2021.

So, let’s just say that—being a nature-lover myself—I was psyched about tackling this project. I always say that Mother Nature is my favorite artist, and here was a chance to recreate one of her most beautiful creatures in cake. Game on!

While Glenn built the basic display—the flooring and a back wall—I reached out to my contacts at Satin Ice ([www.satinice.com](http://www.satinice.com)), The Sweet Chalet Shoppe ([www.the sweetchalet.com](http://www.the sweetchalet.com)), Icing Images ([www.icingimages.com](http://www.icingimages.com)), and The Sugar Art ([www.thesugarart.com](http://www.thesugarart.com)) for supplies. They were all intrigued by the project and happy to get me the products I needed.

Soon, Glenn dropped off the basic display. We added casters and rolled everything into my dining room.

And then, it was time for me to go to work.

I prefabricated the structure for shrubbery and tree branches out of PVC pipes and wires. I also pre-made rocks out of rice treats covered in fondant. I then crafted antlers out of wire wrapped in tin foil and covered in modeling chocolate and fondant.

On Glenn’s base, I built a strong skeleton—which included the two front legs, shoulders, back, belly, neck and skull—out of food-grade PVC pipe and some wood. I added layers of cake, then shaped them to form the body.

Slowly, I covered the structure in modeling chocolate “muscle,” shaping it as I went along. Next, I hollowed out sockets for the eyes and molded the snout.

The skin is actually fondant, which I added in thin layers and textured. Then, I placed my prefabricated rocks and branches in strategic places around the base. I added the eyes made out of gumpaste, and began spot-painting the deer with edible paints.

Next, I attached the antlers to the head. I added ears and covered the entire head and antlers with a thin layer of fondant, which I sculpted and textured.

For me, a piece like this one really comes alive when the painting starts. It took a while, but I hand-painted everything—the deer, the rocks, the shrubs, the branches, and the grass (which was actually an edible moss I made). I also whipped up some leaves out of wafer paper and attached them to the display.

In all, it took two weeks to complete. I “boxed” it up with furring strips and plastic sheeting, awaiting the day Glenn would pick it up and cart it off to the celebration. But my adventure wasn’t over.

On the appointed morning, our friend Jim Housman came over to help us load up the piece into Glenn’s van. But when Glenn rolled by to pick up the cake, we discovered that something had been mismeasured—the deer would not entirely fit! Now what?

We gingerly manipulated the piece in through the swinging doors. I can tell you, it was not light. Cake is heavier than most people think to begin with, and when you added Glenn’s solid wooden base, we had a tough time. But we managed to get 90 percent of the deer in, then closed up the doors over the backside as best we could with ratchet straps.

I hopped in with Glenn and we did pretty well on the trip—until we hit 17B. It was there that an evil pothole intervened and the rear of the deer display jerked back hard.

Luckily, the ratchet straps held. We managed to get the deer to the hunting preserve where six other guys helped Jim extract the piece and set it in place. I had brought an “emergency kit” that consisted of modeling chocolate, fondant and edible paints with me. After a touch-up or two, the deer looked great. The people attending the party were blown away, and I was genuinely flattered to receive a ton of sincere compliments.

Now, I know that making a life-sized deer is beyond the scope of our usual projects for Kim’s Kitchen, so I certainly don’t expect readers to go all Dr. Frankenstein and try to create one of these. I just thought it would be cool to show what’s possible as you develop your skills in food and cake art. Working on smaller projects will expand your knowledge and confidence, enabling you to work up to bigger and more realistic pieces. Just remember that you’re not in a race with anyone but yourself.

Oh, and don’t forget to measure your piece accurately, or it might not get out the door!

<Youtube id="nPs3WjD5gmY" title="How And Why I Made A Life sized Deer Cake" client:load />
```


make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate.mdx:

```---
title: "Make a beautiful rose out of modeling chocolate"
date: 2020-04-21
draft: true
description: "It’s safe to say that this past month has been a challenge. I hope everyone reading this is safe and making the best of the situation. If you’re one of those people who is considered …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate,37079"
image: "@assets/blog/make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200421-160835-17fd-kims-_Pink_Rose.jpg"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200421-160835-17fd-kims-_Pink_Rose.jpg';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200421-161105-Pink_20Rose_201.jpg';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200421-161110-Pink_20Rose_202.jpg';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200421-161113-Pink_20Rose_203.jpg';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200421-161117-Pink_20Rose_204.jpg';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200421-161120-Pink_20Rose_205.jpg';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200421-161123-Pink_20Rose_206.jpg';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200421-161127-Pink_20Rose_207.jpg';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200421-161130-Pink_20Rose_208.jpg';
import img10 from '@assets/blog/make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200421-161134-Pink_20Rose_209.jpg';
import img11 from '@assets/blog/make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200421-161138-Pink_20Rose_2010.jpg';
import img12 from '@assets/blog/make-a-beautiful-rose-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200421-161142-Pink_20Rose_2011.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

It’s safe to say that this past month has been a challenge. I hope everyone reading this is safe and making the best of the situation.

If you’re one of those people who is considered an essential worker, you have my thanks... especially if you’re in healthcare! If you’ve been laid off because of this crisis, I can only hope that better days are to come.

Being my own boss, I fall into the work-from-home category, which isn’t much different from my norm. I did have a bunch of public events canceled—cake shows in Virginia and Florida, a slew of private classes and my monthly Sip-and-Paints at Hector’s Inn over in Bethel, NY. But I’ve been lucky enough to have jobs still coming in.

Just before writing this column, I was walking outside in my yard. In spite of how wiggy the weather has been, some of the plants are starting to bud. Nothing, it seems, can stop them. Seeing this gave me a moment of hope.

That’s why this month’s Kim’s Kitchen features a rose. Roses carry a lot of symbolic power in our culture, including the concepts of friendship and love. Now is certainly a good time to try to focus on those ideas.

Also, making a rose—and topping a delicious cupcake with it—is an ideal project to do as a family when you’re pretty much staying home. All the materials can be ordered online, and the smell of cupcakes baking is one of the best there is.

Please stay hopeful and strong.

<Youtube id="DfM54nM_dKc" title="YouTube video" client:load />

**Materials**:

modeling chocolate (I prefer Satin Ice Choco Pan)
plastic clay tool (for smoothing)
corn starch or powdered sugar
toothpick
petal dust (I prefer the Sugar Art Colors)
piping bag and tip
buttercream
sprinkles
cupcakes
small paintbrush



<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Soften modeling chocolate (knead until smooth). Make a ball (approximately 1-inch in diameter) for the base of the rose. Make four 1/4-inch balls for the first set of petals. Make three 1/2-inch balls for the second set of petals. Make five 3/4-inch balls for the third set of petals.



<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Pinch the 1/4-inch balls until flat, then pinch the edges to add texture.



<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Take first petal, attach to 1-inch ball, and form a cone (this may require a little water).

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Flip petal over and press thumb into the center to shape it.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Take a flattened petal, curve it as shown, and roll the opposite edges with a toothpick.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Start adding petals one at a time, keeping the height a tiny bit higher the center cone. Overlap each petal with the petal next to it. Use smoothing tool to attach. Using your fingers, curl back the tips of the petal to give it more dimension.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Repeat the process using the three 1/2-inch balls for the second set of petals and using the five 3/4-inch balls for the third set of petals. Continue until you have a rose the size you desire!



<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img9} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Dust rose with petal dust to add dimension (I prefer The Sugar Art Colors)

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img10} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Pipe buttercream onto the cupcake

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img11} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Add sprinkles

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img12} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Add rose to cupcake
```


make-a-cookie-out-of-your-puppy.mdx:

```---
title: "Make a cookie out of your puppy"
date: 2024-05-22
description: "Yes, my four-and-a-half-pound Maltese dog is named after Ozzy Osbourne. I can’t help myself. I love both Ozzies. The story of my Ozzy is a bittersweet one. My friends Yvonne and Jim …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags:
  [
    cookie-decorating,
    sugar-cookies,
    pet-portraits,
    dog-art,
    autism-awareness,
    diy-cookies,
    baking-with-friends,
    gift-ideas,
    beginner-baking,
  ]
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-a-cookie-out-of-your-puppy,148705"
image: "@assets/blog/make-a-cookie-out-of-your-puppy/dogs-and-cookies.jpg"
imageAlt: "Two people each holding a small white dog, with decorated pet-shaped sugar cookies displayed in front"
showFeaturedImage: false
---

import { Image } from "astro:assets";
import img1 from "@assets/blog/make-a-cookie-out-of-your-puppy/dogs-and-cookies.jpg";
import img2 from "@assets/blog/make-a-cookie-out-of-your-puppy/cookies.jpg";

Yes, my four-and-a-half-pound Maltese dog is named after Ozzy Osbourne. I can’t help myself. I love both Ozzies.

The story of my Ozzy is a bittersweet one. My friends Yvonne and Jim Housman lost their son Devin on June 6, 2020. Devin was autistic, and his family has since set up an academic scholarship in his memory.

This is very close to my heart, since my grandson Riley is autistic as well. (For more information about the scholarship, click and/or copy and paste this link: www.cfosny.org/our-funds/scholarships/devin-michael-housman-memorial-scholarship-fund/).

Shortly after Devin’s death, a Maltese pup came into Yvonne and Jim’s lives. Being Disney fans, they named her Jasmine (after the princess in “Aladdin”). They were amazed when they discovered that Jasmine was born the very day that Devin had passed.

Maybe Devin had sensed their need and sent this special puppy to them?

In any case, Jasmine is sweet, affectionate, intelligent and beautiful. When she had puppies of her own, Yvonne and Jim gifted us with the runt, which I promptly named—you guessed it—Ozzy. Like his mom, Ozzy is sweet, affectionate, intelligent and beautiful. So much so that I didn’t even mind when Ozzy ruined my birthday dinner this year by escaping from his pen at our friend Joyce’s house in Florida. As soon as I saw on the Ozzy Cam I had set up that he had cleverly set himself free, the fancy restaurant plans sailed out the window—and I sailed down I-595 to make sure he didn’t hurt himself.

Yes, I am that doggie mama. Sure, I probably spoil him just a little. But I know I’m not alone in this.

Take, for example, my friend, the River Reporter’s very own Jonathan Charles Fox. He pals around with “That Dog Named Gidget.” She’s a little spoiled as well, having her own Facebook page, which—as of this writing—is followed by 605 people. (Don’t worry. Ozzy has a Facebook page, too: Ozzy Tales.)

<figure class="blog-image">
  <Image
    src={img1}
    alt="Two people each holding a small white dog, with decorated pet-shaped sugar cookies displayed in front"
  />
  <figcaption>
    Kim poses with Jonathan, their dogs and their cookies. Not the dogs'
    cookies. The cookies of their dogs. Try this month's projects and make
    cookies of your pets too.
  </figcaption>
</figure>

As I write, Gidget has just had her first birthday. She came into Jonathan’s life shortly after the devastating loss of his previous companion, the famous and fabulous Dharma the Wonder Dog. Gidget is a Havanese, which is the only breed originating in Cuba. The Havanese and Maltese breeds are related, and both are hypoallergenic since they have hair rather than fur—so maybe Ozzy and Gidget are cousins!

Whatever the case may be, Jonathan raised Gidget from a puppy.

“She was bound for future service-dog training when I got her,” Jonathan will tell you. “Starting with a puppy seemed the way to go. But that thing about hindsight being 20-20? I sure wish I were clairvoyant!”

It wasn’t easy at first. Jonathan has always had dogs, but he hadn’t trained a puppy in a long, long time.

“She was a rambunctious juvenile who needed constant supervision,” he explains with a sigh. “Plus, she was always clenching strange things in her jaws. And then there was the fact that she wanted to walk anywhere between five and 50 times a day!”

And yet as time went on, Jonathan and That Dog Named Gidget formed an inseparable bond.

“We strolled a lot more as the pooch grew,” Jonathan muses. “I leashed her up morning, noon and night, traversing the neighborhood at all hours, rain or shine. Every day, we could be spotted on the roads near Camp Fox [Jonathan’s home] learning how to heel, sit, and stay. We made good use of the time. I managed to teach her some manners, and we learned new things together about this wonderful world we live in. I’d say walking the dog was good for us both. Of course, there was her brief but all-consuming obsession with tracking down salamanders…”

Gidget is now a full-blown service dog. She happily accompanies Jonathan wherever he goes, always keeping him under her watchful eye. Recently, Gidget accompanied Jonathan to our house. The reason? A play date with Ozzy, of course. Need I say that they got along famously?

More importantly, we got together to create a sample piece for this month’s project—a cookie that looks like Gidget.

<figure class="blog-image">
  <Image
    src={img2}
    alt="Two intricately decorated sugar cookies shaped and designed to look like small white dogs including a Maltese and a Havanese"
  />
  <figcaption>
    Yes, the cookie version of Gidget—and of Ozzy—are fantastic. But more
    importantly, Jonathan was able to plow through his doubts and fears—with
    characteristic humor, of course—and tap into the inner cookie decorator he
    previously didn’t know even existed. Such cookies make great gifts. What do
    you get the person who has everything? Why, you bake a sugar cookie and
    decorate it to look like Sprinkles, their pet Yorkie. This is not a present
    people receive every day!
  </figcaption>
</figure>

Of course, I’d already made a cookie version of Ozzy. (Yes, I am still that doggie mama.) When I decided to teach someone else how to do the same with their pet, I knew that Jonathan would be the ideal participant. True—as you’ll see in the video—he repeatedly noted that he had no prior baking or cookie decorating experience whatsoever. But I knew he loved Gidget enough to give it a go anyway and work his way through his fears.

<Youtube
  id="AvfGpJ8UTk4"
  title="Cookie Out of Your Puppy video tutorial"
  client:load
/>

And that’s probably the greatest thing that came out of our collaboration. Yes, the cookie version of Gidget is fantastic. But more importantly, Jonathan was able to plow through his doubts and fears—with characteristic humor, of course—and tap into the inner cookie decorator he previously didn’t know even existed.

Such cookies make great gifts. What do you get the person who has everything? Why, you bake a sugar cookie and decorate it to look like Sprinkles, their pet Yorkie. This is not a present people receive every day!

And the process of creating that gift? Well, it can be frustrating—at first. But be willing to make mistakes. Playing it safe won’t teach you much. Get out of the box. If you’re like me, you’ll build your own box and dance around the outside.

And remember to have fun. Ozzy and Gidget would expect no less.
```


make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie.mdx:

```---
title: "Make a double-stuffed farm animal cookie"
date: 2022-05-26
draft: true
description: "Anyone who knows anything about me knows that I love animals. And I’m proud that the same love of animals has also manifested in my kids. My son Corey, for example, is a 6-foot-2-inch …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie,55644"
image: "@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122100-21fd-_Kim-_cookie_main.jpg"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122100-21fd-_Kim-_cookie_main.jpg';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122108-Farm_20Animal_20Cookie_201.jpg';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122111-Farm_20Animal_20Cookie_202.jpg';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122113-Farm_20Animal_20Cookie_203.jpg';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122116-Farm_20Animal_20Cookie_204.jpg';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122118-Farm_20Animal_20Cookie_205.jpg';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122120-Farm_20Animal_20Cookie_207.jpg';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122121-Farm_20Animal_20Cookie_208.jpg';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122123-Farm_20Animal_20Cookie_209.jpg';
import img10 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122125-Farm_20Animal_20Cookie_2010.jpg';
import img11 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122126-Farm_20Animal_20Cookie_2011.jpg';
import img12 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122129-Farm_20Animal_20Cookie_2012.jpg';
import img13 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122131-Farm_20Animal_20Cookie_2013.jpg';
import img14 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122133-Farm_20Animal_20Cookie_2014.jpg';
import img15 from '@assets/blog/make-a-double-stuffed-farm-animal-cookie/20220526-122103-Double_20Stuffed_20Farm_20Animal_20Supply_20List.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        The completed Double-Stuffed Farm Animal cookies.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Anyone who knows anything about me knows that I love animals. And I’m proud that the same love of animals has also manifested in my kids.

My son Corey, for example, is a 6-foot-2-inch marathon runner. He’s also a vegetarian who will let a spider loose in the wild rather than kill it in the house.

However, my daughter Kayliegh has really taken it to the next level. When the COVID-19 lockdowns began, Kayliegh—like a lot of people—was concerned. How long would this last? What about food?

She and her husband Jason have a few acres, so Kayliegh decided to start a small homestead. She began with chickens for eggs—and is currently getting 19 per day.

But what really changed things for her was the addition of Nigerian dwarf goats. My grandson Riley is autistic, but he bonded with these goats immediately—so much so that his doctor has dubbed them Riley’s “therapy goats.” (Dean, my other grandson, loves them as well.)

Being resilient, Kayliegh has begun making and selling wonderful soap out of goat’s milk. Her clients are thrilled, saying that the soap is uniquely gentle and moisturizing. You can look it up on her website, [https://chickenhartehomest.wixsite.com/autismgoats](https://chickenhartehomest.wixsite.com/autismgoats).

Kayliegh and Jason have also begun breeding and selling Nigerian dwarf goats themselves, with part of the proceeds going to the Devin Michael Housman Memorial Scholarship Fund, a charity set up to benefit local students with special needs. You can read about this very worthy charity at [https://cfosny.org/our-funds/scholarships/devin-michael-housman-memorial-scholarship-fund/](https://cfosny.org/our-funds/scholarships/devin-michael-housman-memorial-scholarship-fund/).

That’s why I thought these Double-Stuffed Farm Animal cookies would be a good project for this month. They’re fairly easy to make, they’re cute and delicious, and they’ll certainly be a hit whenever you serve them.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Roll out sugar cookie dough.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Shape top dough with mold and cut excess dough from shape.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Roll out sugar cookie dough for bottom and trim dough to shape.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Bake 8-10 minutes at 375 degrees Fahrenheit.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Remove molds from the top cookies.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Using a piping bag, coat inside of top with buttercream icing and add cake. Cover cake with chocolate ganache.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Put top and bottom cookies together.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img9} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Coat body with piping gel using a brush.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img10} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Roll out fondant thinly and cover the cookie; you now have the body.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img11} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Roll out fondant thickly, about 1/4 inch for the head. Using a round cookie cutter, cut out the head. Using a ball tool, add the face details.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img12} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Add more detail to face/body with darker fondant and glue with piping gel.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img13} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Shape horns, feet, eyes, nose, ears, etc. from fondant and glue with piping gel.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img14} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Cut or bite your cookie and enjoy!
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img15} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Double-Stuffed Farm Animal Cookie Steps:

- Roll out sugar cookie dough.
- Shape top dough with mold and cut excess dough from shape.
- Roll out sugar cookie dough for bottom and trim dough to shape.
- Bake 8-10 minutes at 375 degrees Fahrenheit.
- Remove mold from the top cookies.
- Using a piping bag, coat inside of top with buttercream icing and add cake.
- Cover cake with chocolate ganache.
- Put top and bottom cookies together.
- Coat body with piping gel using a brush.
- Roll out fondant thinly and cover the cookie; you now have the body.
- Roll out fondant thickly about ¼ inch for head. Using a round cookie cutter, cut out the head. Using a ball tool, add the face details.
- Add more detail to face/body with darker fondant and glue with piping gel.
- Shape horns, feet, eyes, nose, ears…etc. from fondant and glue with piping gel.
- Cut or bite your cookie and enjoy!

<Youtube id="gjIo_KI5p0w" title="YouTube video player" client:load />
```


make-a-valentine-s-day-lovebird-cake-topper-out-of-modeling-chocolate.mdx:

```---
title: "Make a Valentine’s Day lovebird cake topper out of modeling chocolate"
date: 2020-01-22
draft: true
description: "A lot of people look at what I do and say, “I could never do that.” While that might be true on a professional level, that doesn’t mean that you can’t let your creative spirit …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-a-valentines-day-lovebird-cake-topper-out-of-modeling-chocolate,35418"
image: "@assets/blog/make-a-valentine-s-day-lovebird-cake-topper-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200122-094844-Love_Birds_Cake_Topper.jpg"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/make-a-valentine-s-day-lovebird-cake-topper-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200122-094844-Love_Birds_Cake_Topper.jpg';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/make-a-valentine-s-day-lovebird-cake-topper-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200122-094232-1.jpg';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/make-a-valentine-s-day-lovebird-cake-topper-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200122-094238-2.jpg';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/make-a-valentine-s-day-lovebird-cake-topper-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200122-094241-3.jpg';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/make-a-valentine-s-day-lovebird-cake-topper-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200122-094248-4.jpg';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-a-valentine-s-day-lovebird-cake-topper-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200122-094254-5.jpg';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-a-valentine-s-day-lovebird-cake-topper-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200122-094301-6.jpg';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-a-valentine-s-day-lovebird-cake-topper-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200122-094308-7.jpg';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/make-a-valentine-s-day-lovebird-cake-topper-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200122-094314-8.jpg';
import img10 from '@assets/blog/make-a-valentine-s-day-lovebird-cake-topper-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200122-094320-9.jpg';
import img11 from '@assets/blog/make-a-valentine-s-day-lovebird-cake-topper-out-of-modeling-chocolate/20200122-094328-10.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

A lot of people look at what I do and say, “I could never do that.” While that might be true on a professional level, that doesn’t mean that you can’t let your creative spirit loose in the kitchen. If competing on Food Network TV shows has taught me anything, it’s that wonders can come out of pressure—if you’re willing to open yourself up to possibilities.

Of course, a few tips can’t hurt. And that’s one of the reasons I’m writing these “Kim’s Kitchen” tutorials. I’ve picked up a lot over the years, and it’s always nice to give back to people who have supported me. And who knows? Maybe I’ll be helping someone become a Food Network champ in the future!

<Youtube id="aV2e4Wnw4F0" title="YouTube video" client:load />

So let’s begin with this cute lovebird cake topper: a perfect touch for the Valentine’s Day season. Here are the tools and materials you’ll need:

silicon mat
ball tool
plastic clay tool (for smoothing)
small scissors
small paintbrush
corn starch or powdered sugar
colored modeling chocolate
white gumpaste
petal dust
water

Once you’ve collected everything, follow this step-by-step photo tutorial:



<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Soften some purple, pink, and yellow modeling chocolate (knead until smooth)

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Make a ball out of the purple modeling chocolate (approximately 1 1/2 inches in diameter) for the body of the bird.

Shape into a teardrop shape.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Cut a one-inch slit in the tail and separate it slightly. Make one ball out of the purple modeling chocolate (approximately 1/8-inch in diameter) for the feather on top of the tail.




<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Make one ball out of the purple modeling chocolate (approximately 1-inch in diameter) for the birds’s head.

Shape it into a cone, then—using the ball tool—make holes for the eyes and the beak.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Make two balls out of white gumpaste (approximately 1/8-inch in diameter) for the bird’s eyes. Attach using a little water on a brush.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>


Make one ball with the yellow modeling chocolate (approximately 1/4-inch in diameter) for the bird’s beak. Shape it using the clay tool and attach it to the head with a little water.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Attach the head to the body using a little water and a toothpick.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img9} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Make two balls out of the purple modeling chocolate (approximately ( 1/2-inch in diameter) for the wings. Cut slits for feathers, and—using a clay tool—add details. Attach to the body using a smoothing tool and a little water.




<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img10} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Make one ball with the yellow modeling chocolate (approximately 1/4-inch in diameter) for the bird’s feet; shape them using a clay tool. Attach to the body using a smoothing tool.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img11} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Paint in (or use edible markers) eye details, and—using petal dust and a small brush—add shadows.

Then, guess what? You get to do it again with the pink modeling chocolate for Lovebird #2!

Remember—practice makes perfect, but always try to have fun. And if others compliment you on your beautiful lovebirds, don’t feel like you have to point out your mistakes—just thank them from the heart!
```


make-an-abstract-snowflake-cookie.mdx:

```---
title: "Make an abstract snowflake cookie"
date: 2020-11-25
draft: true
description: "If you’ve ever seen an image of a snowflake under a microscope, you know how amazingly beautiful they are. Individually or in clusters, snowflakes rival any artwork created by …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-an-abstract-snowflake-cookie,40728"
image: "@assets/blog/make-an-abstract-snowflake-cookie/20201125-103524-48fd-_kim-_main.JPG"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/make-an-abstract-snowflake-cookie/20201125-103524-48fd-_kim-_main.JPG';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/make-an-abstract-snowflake-cookie/20201125-094124-Abstract_20SnowFlake_20Cookie_20Supply_20list.JPG';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/make-an-abstract-snowflake-cookie/20201125-094102-Abstract_20SnowFlake_20Cookie_201.JPG';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/make-an-abstract-snowflake-cookie/20201125-094104-Abstract_20SnowFlake_20Cookie_202.JPG';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/make-an-abstract-snowflake-cookie/20201125-094113-Abstract_20SnowFlake_20Cookie_203.jpg';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-an-abstract-snowflake-cookie/20201125-094115-Abstract_20SnowFlake_20Cookie_204.JPG';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-an-abstract-snowflake-cookie/20201125-094117-Abstract_20SnowFlake_20Cookie_205.JPG';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-an-abstract-snowflake-cookie/20201125-094119-Abstract_20SnowFlake_20Cookie_206.JPG';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/make-an-abstract-snowflake-cookie/20201125-094121-Abstract_20SnowFlake_20Cookie_207.JPG';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Learn how to make your own abstract cookie at www.riverreporter.com/kims-kitchen.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

If you’ve ever seen an image of a snowflake under a microscope, you know how amazingly beautiful they are. Individually or in clusters, snowflakes rival any artwork created by humankind.

Growing up, I always heard that no two snowflakes were alike. Given the number of snowflakes that fall during a storm—and the fact that I naturally question everything—I wondered, “How can that be true?” Turns out, there's an answer:

“The probability that two snow crystals (a single ice crystal) or flakes (a snow crystal or multiple snow crystals stuck together) will be exactly alike in molecular structure and in appearance, is very minute... First, we need to understand that not all water molecules are exactly alike...Furthermore, the unique and complex features of snow crystals are very much affected by unstable atmospheric conditions. Snow crystals are sensitive to temperature and will change in shape and design as they fall from the cloud and are exposed to fluctuating temperatures. To have two snow crystals or flakes with the same history of development is virtually impossible” (www.loc.gov).

I guess that makes sense to me. But more importantly, it hits me that snowflakes are the perfect example of abstract art.

When I was a kid, I have to admit I had no appreciation for abstract art. Looking back, this was mostly because my family would talk it down whenever we’d see it in a museum: “What’s this supposed to be? How can anyone like it?” The idea seemed to be that if they didn’t understand it, it couldn’t be any good.

But later on, artists like Jackson Pollack opened my eyes. Abstract art is anything but random; it has purpose and design. And it can be beautiful.

That’s what makes this month’s project so inspiring for me. In designing your own abstract snowflake cookie, you can actually participate in abstract art in a way that’s fun and has purpose: it’s a perfect way to unite abstract art with food art!

And who doesn’t love the smell of delicious cookies baking at this time of year?

<Youtube id="j0S-85ETqcw" title="YouTube video" client:load />

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Materials needed.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Split the Royal Icing into 4 small bowls. Color the Royal Icing using gel colors of your choice, and stir with a spoon.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

To check for the correct consistency for flooding, take some icing on a spatula and drop it back into the bowl. If it sinks after a full count of 10, then the icing is thin enough for flooding.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Place the pastry bag over a tall glass and "cuff" the excess over the edge. Fill the pastry bag 1/2 to 2/3 full.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Squeeze the filled pastry bag to get out any air bubbles, then twist and tie off the end.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Repeat this process for all the colors.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Cradle the pastry bag against your palm, between your thumb and rest of your fingers (wrap your fingers around it and squeeze to control the flow of icing). Start by outlining your cookie. Keep the icing about 1/8–to–1/4 of an inch from the edge (slightly more if you plan to do a border).

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img9} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Once you've outlined your cookies, you'll use the "flood" icing. Use a scribe or a toothpick to pull colors together.
```


make-some-delicious-corned-beef.mdx:

```---
title: "Make some delicious corned beef"
date: 2021-02-24
recipe: ./corned-beef
description: "Growing up, my dad loved to cook for our family. A decorated Korean War veteran, he and my mom were very involved in the Liberty, NY Veterans of Foreign Wars, cooking for all sorts of events. He …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-some-delicious-corned-beef,42054"
image: "@assets/recipes/corned-beef/corned-beef.jpg"
showFeaturedImage: false
---

Growing up, my dad loved to cook for our family. A decorated Korean War veteran, he and my mom were very involved in the Liberty, NY Veterans of Foreign Wars, cooking for all sorts of events. He often used those catering skills at home, as well.

Among my dad’s most secret recipes was one for corned beef/brisket. As kids, we were sworn to secrecy about how, exactly, Dad’s corned beef was made.

So, imagine my surprise when I discovered that, only four short years ago, my dad’s secret recipe wasn’t actually his at all!

It turns out that Ma Rinehart, a longtime family friend, was the originator.

<RecipeEmbed recipeId="corned-beef" />

And while we’re tackling corned-beef myths, we might as well debunk the idea that corned beef and cabbage is the way they celebrate St. Patrick’s day in Ireland.

“Truth be told, corned beef and cabbage is about as Irish as a McDonald’s Shamrock Shake,” writes Debbie Lord with Cox Media Group National Content Desk. “What has become a tradition of eating corned beef and cabbage to celebrate St. Patrick’s Day likely grew out of the fact that those foods were less expensive for immigrants who came to America. They substituted beef for pork and cabbage for potatoes,” (www.bit.ly/kimstpatty08).

Now, would you like to know how good this corned beef tastes?

I have a series of food allergies, particularly to gluten. I’m always saying that I’m the cake artist who can’t eat cake!

But I also have difficulty digesting red meat, a condition that developed after I was bitten by a tick a few years ago.

Nevertheless, whenever I make this corned beef recipe, I’m always tempted to chow down on a plateful, no matter what distress I might be facing later. That’s how good this is—so good that it shouldn’t be kept secret anymore.

Make some for yourself. I bet you’ll agree!

<Youtube id="3tdKXpajB6s" title="YouTube video" client:load />
```


make-sugar-like-glass.mdx:

```---
title: "Make sugar like glass"
date: 2021-09-22
draft: true
description: "I&rsquo;ve always loved to sculpt, and this is the skill that has primarily gotten me into most of my competitions on Food Network. But while shooting &ldquo;Holiday Wars&rdquo; in 2019, I became &hellip;"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-sugar-like-glass,48212"
image: "@assets/blog/make-sugar-like-glass/20210922-121732-38FD-_Kim_online-_Blue_Wonder.jpg"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/make-sugar-like-glass/20210922-121732-38FD-_Kim_online-_Blue_Wonder.jpg';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/make-sugar-like-glass/20210922-121740-Blue_20Wonder_20Supply_20List.jpg';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/make-sugar-like-glass/20210922-121719-38FD-_20Kim_20online-_201.jpg';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/make-sugar-like-glass/20210922-121721-38FD-_20Kim_20online-_202.jpg';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/make-sugar-like-glass/20210922-121723-38FD-_20Kim_20online-_203.jpg';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-sugar-like-glass/20210922-121725-38FD-_20Kim_20online-_204.jpg';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-sugar-like-glass/20210922-121725-38FD-_20Kim_20online-_205.jpg';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-sugar-like-glass/20210922-121726-38FD-_20Kim_20online-_206.jpg';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/make-sugar-like-glass/20210922-121728-38FD-_20Kim_20online-_207.jpg';
import img10 from '@assets/blog/make-sugar-like-glass/20210922-121729-38FD-_20Kim_20online-_208.jpg';
import img11 from '@assets/blog/make-sugar-like-glass/20210922-121732-38FD-_20Kim_20online-_20Blue_20Wonder.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        The final Blue Wonder.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

I’ve always loved to sculpt, and this is the skill that has primarily gotten me into most of my competitions on Food Network. But while shooting “Holiday Wars” in 2019, I became fascinated by what our team’s sugar artist was accomplishing.

Roberto Nieto comes from wine country in Northern California. He was nicknamed Chef Buttercup many moons ago, but when I saw his work with our team—The Bah Hum Bakers—I quickly renamed him The Chocolate Ninja. Robert was a big reason why we won “Holiday Wars” in December of that year.

When COVID-19 hit, I spent a great deal of time teaching myself some of the skills I saw Robert use on the show. Luckily, I’m close friends with Sidney Galpern, who has the SimiCakes shop in Florida. Sidney supplied me with many raw materials. Before long, I was making sugar look like glass. And the Blue Wonder sugar piece was born.

I’ve always loved the look of sapphire. It symbolizes energy and healing. The ancients believed that sapphire offered spiritual insight, protection, and good luck. Who doesn’t need that?

The primary material for this project is isomalt—a sugar substitute made from beets. Isomalt needs to be heated to very high temperatures in a microwave to be used properly, so I recommend caution—and gloves.

A butane torch is also required, so I would say that a skill level beyond “beginner” is best if you choose to tackle it. The results, though, are well worth the effort. As always, feel free to experiment with any colors/shapes that you would like. It’s always preferable to let your creativity take charge!

Sadly, though isomalt is edible, it’s not really good for your digestive system in mass quantities. Moderation is best there. Also, like many things that are beautiful, your sugar-like-glass pieces won’t last very long. Humidity is a particular enemy. But also like so many things that are beautiful, the temporary joy is worth the short lifespan.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        1. Sketch your design on a SmArt Sheet (from Icing Images).
    </figcaption>
</figure>

1. Sketch your design on a SmArt Sheet (from Icing Images)

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        2. Paint your design with edible colors (I prefer Artisan Accents).
    </figcaption>
</figure>

2. Paint your design with edible colors (I prefer Artisan Accents)

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        3. Heat the blue isomalt in the microwave. Note: heat for 30 seconds, then in 15-second intervals, until it bubbles. Caution: isomalt is HOT. Gloves are recommended.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

3. Heat the blue isomalt in the microwave. Note: heat for 30 seconds, then in 15-second intervals, until it bubbles. Caution: isomalt is HOT. Gloves are recommended.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        4-7. Surround your SmArt Sheet design with a Flex Form (I prefer SimiCakes). Remove design and pour blue isomalt into Flex Form. Let cool. Clear bubbles on isomalt with a blow torch. Place design on blue isomalt.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

4. Surround your SmArt Sheet design with a Flex Form (I prefer SimiCakes).

5. Remove design and pour blue isomalt into Flex Form. Let cool.

6. Clear bubbles on isomalt with a blow torch.

7. Place design on blue isomalt.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        8. Heat up clear isomalt and pour evenly over the design. Let cool.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

8. Heat up clear isomalt and pour evenly over the design. Let cool.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        9. Use torch to clean up any bubbles.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

9. Use torch to clean up any bubbles.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img9} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        10. Heat up isomalt and pour into base mold. Let cool.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

10. Heat up isomalt and pour into base mold. Let cool.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img10} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        11-12. Remove Flex Form from design. Remove base from the mold. Shine both pieces with torch, and attach base with heated isomalt.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

11. Remove Flex Form from design. Remove base from the mold.

12. Shine both pieces with torch, and attach base with heated isomalt.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img11} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        The final Blue Wonder.
    </figcaption>
</figure>
```

---

import { Image } from "astro:assets";
import img1 from "@assets/blog/make-these-chocolate-balloon-cups/balloon-cup-stuff.jpg";
import img2 from "@assets/blog/make-these-chocolate-balloon-cups/chocolate-balloon-cups.jpg";
