make-this-candle-cake-centerpiece.mdx:

```---
title: "Make this candle cake centerpiece"
date: 2024-11-27
description: "Something happened while I snuck up on people as part of Burn Brae Mansion’s Dark Forest Fright Walk in Glen Spey, NY this past October… The holiday season snuck up on me! …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags:
  [
    holiday-centerpiece,
    cake,
    candle-cake,
    edible-decor,
    winter-solstice,
    diy-decor,
    holiday-baking,
    table-decor,
    edible-crafts,
  ]
author: kim-simons
recipe: ./chocolate-ganache
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-this-candle-cake-centerpiece,173418"
image: "@assets/blog/make-this-candle-cake-centerpiece/candle-cake.jpg"
imageAlt: "A white candle cake centerpiece decorated to look like a large candle"
showFeaturedImage: false
---

import { Image } from "astro:assets";
import img1 from "@assets/blog/make-this-candle-cake-centerpiece/candle-cake.jpg";

<figure class="blog-image">
  <Image
    src={img1}
    alt="A white candle cake centerpiece decorated to look like a large candle"
  />
  <figcaption>The completed candle cake centerpiece</figcaption>
</figure>

Something happened while I snuck up on people as part of Burn Brae Mansion’s Dark Forest Fright Walk in Glen Spey, NY this past October…

The holiday season snuck up on me!

That’s right: While I was creepily approaching unsuspecting visitors to the Dark Forest in my role as a demented nurse (seeking the right body parts, of course), time was quietly lurking in the background, relentlessly ticking away.

And when I finally turned around, I found myself knee deep in the holiday season once more. How did that happen?

And where did the light go? I thought I’d heard something about the Powers That Be deciding to keep us on Daylight Savings Time from now on—what happened there? Ah, well.

In any case—like it or not—the holidays have indeed arrived, and it’s no good pretending they haven’t—no matter how kind that might actually be on our wallets.

Still, I have to say, one thing about this time of year that brings me joy is the abundance of beautiful, multi-colored lights.

Growing up, my holiday decorating centered on lights. And why not? My mother was born on Christmas Day, so the tree always sparkled brightly—and was forever topped with her favorite angel.

And though my dad was Jewish, he loved Christmas as well. Plus, we decorated for Hanukkah—which is, after all, the Festival of Lights—so there was barely a spot in the house that wasn’t illuminated to the nines, radiating a holiday joy that pierced the darkness of 4:30 p.m.

This is why our project this month is a candle cake centerpiece. I thought it would be really great if we could have a candle to brighten up our holiday table for family and friends—and then be able to eat it, too.

![A list of ingredients and supplies for making the candle cake centerpiece](@assets/blog/make-this-candle-cake-centerpiece/supply-list.jpeg)

This project is inspired by my love of decorative lights and my happiness in being around candles in general. Who doesn’t love the comforting aroma of an apple-scented candle? Or a cinnamon one? Now we can take it one step extra and actually taste it, too (just remember to blow out the wick).

It’s also interesting that our candle cake centerpiece taps into a very real connection between us as humans and candlelight. Let’s face it—candles as symbols are present everywhere we turn, promising hope, warmth, mystery, romance and spirituality. This, I think, is why practically everyone loves how they look.

And at this time of year—as the Winter Solstice approaches—candles radiate even more meaning and charm. A quick internet search reveals that folks as long ago as the 16th century lit up candles to symbolize the light and warmth of the sun. The celebrations we participate in at this time of the year—whether Hanukkah, Christmas, Kwanzaa and/or Wiccan Solstice observances—all prominently feature the magic light of candles—and are more comforting to us because of them.

As always, I encourage you to be as creative as you would like to be with your candle cake centerpieces. Experiment with your favorite holiday flavors—pumpkin, candy cane, nutmeg, ginger, orange—and maybe even matzah. There is no right or wrong, and you should feel free to let your imagination run wild.

And that holds for the wicks, too. Though I used an actual candle wick in my demonstration, you can replace that with LED tea lights and/or birthday candles. The sky is the limit.

<Youtube
  id="dEoTfeVMaYE"
  title="Candle Cake Centerpiece video tutorial"
  client:load
/>

And be prepared to experience your own “Is it cake?” moment when you announce to guests that, yes, they can eat the centerpiece! How many holiday gatherings have they attended where something like that has occurred?

It’s a holiday present all by itself!

<RecipeEmbed recipeId="chocolate-ganache" />
```

```---
<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        The candy surprise bunny
    </figcaption>
</figure>

 Anybody who knows me knows that I love animals.

 True, a few of them don’t love me—I have really bad allergies to most dogs. And don’t let me get anywhere near a cat—I respect them from afar, but no amount of allergy meds on earth will help me weather that storm!

 Over the years, I’ve spent my time with a wide variety of animal friends. I’ve lived with reptiles. I’ve lived with hamsters. I lived with a cardinal I rescued that I named Freedom. Currently, I have four hens, three roosters and seven guinea hens. Eggs for days, and no ticks allowed on our portion of Wurtsboro Mountain!

 Our friends Yvonne and Jim Housman have also recently gifted us with a hypoallergenic Maltese pup whom I’ve named Ozzy (yes, after Ozzy Osbourne—he even has a chew toy in the shape of a bat). Ozzy is coming up on 10 weeks old, and may weigh all of two pounds. Of course, he thinks he’s a lion.

Given that we’re coming up on the Passover-Easter season, my thoughts have recently dropped back yet again to my younger years, particularly the days when one of my animal friends was a rabbit—a female with pure-white fur. As it happens, I was babysitting a friend’s rabbit, which happened to be male. Well, somehow, those stealthy bunnies got together because—about 30 days later—she was burrowing into our couch in the back room and giving birth! You know my kids enjoyed playing with those six little fur balls. My daughter Kayliegh was even singing to them.

Now, someone who knows about these things will point out that rabbits and eggs are symbolic of spring, a time when the planet becomes fertile again. (Rabbits and chickens are certainly fertile!)

Me? I just like how cute they are—almost as cute as Ozzy. (Have I mentioned Ozzy?)

That’s why I chose this candy surprise bunny as this month’s project. It’s a little more difficult than some you might have worked through with me here, and it involves some heating with a blow torch, so please be careful. But the results will be well worth it. If there are little kids in your life, wait until you see their smiles when they crack open those chocolate spheres and find candy in them.

As always, make your candy surprise bunny look any way you choose. Experiment with different colors of fondant. Use your favorite candy. Tap into the creative juice that makes you, well, you.

Ozzy would expect nothing less.
```

```---
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-this-chocolate-bomb-snowman,51548"
image: "@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-bomb-snowman/20220126-102014-Chocolate_bomb_Snowman.jpg"

---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-bomb-snowman/20220126-102014-Chocolate_bomb_Snowman.jpg';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-bomb-snowman/20220126-102012-Chocolate_20bomb_20Snowman_20Supplies.jpg';

import img5 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-bomb-snowman/20220126-101959-Chocolate_20bomb_20Snowman_203.jpg';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-bomb-snowman/20220126-102000-Chocolate_20bomb_20Snowman_204.jpg';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-bomb-snowman/20220126-102002-Chocolate_20bomb_20Snowman_205.jpg';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-bomb-snowman/20220126-102003-Chocolate_20bomb_20Snowman_206.jpg';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-bomb-snowman/20220126-102004-Chocolate_20bomb_20Snowman_207.jpg';
import img10 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-bomb-snowman/20220126-102005-Chocolate_20bomb_20Snowman_208.jpg';
import img11 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-bomb-snowman/20220126-102006-Chocolate_20bomb_20Snowman_209.jpg';
import img12 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-bomb-snowman/20220126-102008-Chocolate_20bomb_20Snowman_2011.jpg';
import img13 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-bomb-snowman/20220126-102007-Chocolate_20bomb_20Snowman_2010.jpg';
import img14 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-bomb-snowman/20220126-102010-Chocolate_20bomb_20Snowman_2012.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        The completed Chocolate Bomb Snowman, before he goes in the mug.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Maybe it was a coincidence that we experienced our first significant snowfall of the season as I began to write this column (January 7, to be exact—Russian Orthodox Christmas). And it’s about time, because I’m a big fan of snow people!

I mean, who doesn’t love Sam the Snowman of “Rudolph the Red-Nosed Reindeer” from 1964?

What might be surprising is how long people have been sculpting figures out of snow. It turns out that we’ve been turning snow into freezing art since the Middle Ages. In 1494, even Michelangelo got in the game when he was paid to make a snowman for the ruler of Florence. (The source is Bob Eckstein’s “The Fascinating History of the Snowman” from the internet.)
And what is the very best thing to do after sculpting a frozen masterpiece of your own? Why, having a cup of delicious hot chocolate, of course.

That’s why this chocolate bomb snowman is a great project for this month. You get to make a snowman—inside, so your teeth don’t chatter—and then melt him in hot water for a delicious hot chocolate treat.

See, the secret is creating a chocolate shell in the form of a snowman… and filling it with your favorite hot chocolate. Then drop him in a mug of boiling water big enough to hold him and watch the magic happen.

While this project may be a little more difficult than some of the others we’ve featured in Kim’s Kitchen, the results are well worth it.

Stay warm!

<Youtube id="gZHlDpvj2DE" title="YouTube video player" client:load />

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

1. Pour white chocolate wafers into a glass bowl; reserve a handful on the side.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Pour white chocolate wafers into a glass bowl.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Reserve a handful of wafers on the side.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

2. Heat wafers for 30 seconds in microwave, then at 15-second intervals until it reaches about 83 degrees F. Mix.

3. Stir in reserve wafers to temper.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Heat wafers for 30 seconds in microwave, then at 15-second intervals until it reaches about 83 degrees Fahrenheit. Mix. Stir in reserved wafers to temper.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

4. Coat the interior of two 3" silicone molds with white chocolate; allow to harden.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Coat the interior of two 3-inch silicone molds with white chocolate; allow to harden.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

 5. Coat the interior of two 1 3/4" silicone molds with white chocolate; allow to harden.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Coat the interior of two 1¾- -inch silicone molds with white chocolate; allow to harden.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

 6. Using a piping bag, create stick arms from melted dark chocolate wafers. Do the same for the nose. Once hardened, color with Peach.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Using a piping bag, create stick arms from melted dark chocolate wafers. Do the same for the nose. Once hardened, color with peach coloring.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

 7. Remove 3" spheres from mold; fill one with hot chocolate, and the other with mini marshmallows. Heat the edge of a sphere and assemble.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img9} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Remove 3-inch spheres from mold.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img10} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Fill one sphere with hot chocolate and the other with mini-marshmallows. Heat the edge of a sphere and assemble.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

 8. Using melted white chocolate and paint brushes, add detail to the 3" sphere.

9. Assemble 1 3/4" sphere into head. Attach to body with melted chocolate. Add details with brush.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img11} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Assemble 1 3/4-inch sphere into head. Attach to body with melted chocolate. Add details with brush.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

 10. Attach nose and arms with melted chocolate.

 11. Paint eyes, mouth, and buttons with melted dark chocolate.

 12. Shape feet out of dark chocolate. Attach to body once hardened.

 13. Pour dark chocolate into square or round mold. Allow to harden.

 14. Pour dark chocolate circle about 2" in diameter to form hat brim. Allow to harden.

 15. Attach brim to head with melted chocolate.

 16. Remove square or circle from mold. Attach to brim with melted chocolate.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img12} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Attach nose and arms with melted chocolate. Paint eyes, mouth, and buttons with melted dark chocolate. Shape feet out of dark chocolate. Attach to body once hardened. Pour dark chocolate into square or round mold. Allow to harden. Pour dark chocolate circle about 2 inches in diameter to form hat brim. Allow to harden. Attach brim to head with melted chocolate. Remove square or circle from mold. Attach to brim with melted chocolate.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

 17. Cut SmArt Sheet into strip for scarf. Paint with Hot Red gel. Wrap around snowman's neck.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img13} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Cut SmArt Sheet into strip for scarf. Paint with Hot Red gel. Wrap around snowman's neck.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

 18. Pour boiling water into large mug. Drop snowman in. Stir and enjoy!

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img14} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Pour boiling water into large mug. Drop snowman in. Stir and enjoy!
    </figcaption>
</figure>
```

```---

date: 2024-04-23

url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-this-chocolate-sombrero-piece,144836"

---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-sombrero-piece/20240423-212502-17fd-_Kim-_main.jpeg';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-sombrero-piece/20240423-212514-Materials.jpg';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-sombrero-piece/20240423-212524-Sombrero_20step_201.jpg';

import img5 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-sombrero-piece/20240423-212541-Sombrero_20step_203.jpg';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-sombrero-piece/20240423-212549-Sombrero_20step_204.jpg';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-sombrero-piece/20240423-212558-Sombrero_20step_205.jpg';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-sombrero-piece/20240423-212606-Sombrero_20step_206.jpg';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-sombrero-piece/20240423-212617-Sombrero_20step_207.jpg';
import img10 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-sombrero-piece/20240423-212624-Sombrero_20step_208.jpg';
import img11 from '@assets/blog/make-this-chocolate-sombrero-piece/20240423-212632-Sombrero_20step_209.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        The chocolate sombrero, an homage to El Sombrero Restaurant in Rock Hill, NY.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Temper chocolate wafers: Microwave for 30 seconds, then in 15-second intervals until chocolate reaches about 113 degrees F. Temper with more chocolate wafers until temperature comes down to about 88 degrees. Stir...
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Stir in edible color...
    </figcaption>
</figure>

    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Pour onto a silicone mat supported by upholstery foam. Shape into brim with spatula. Let cool until pliable...
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Trim, then manipulate the silicone mat to shape. Begin the crown by molding and trimming rice treats. ..
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Attach crown to brim with melted chocolate.....
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Roll out modeling chocolate, cover crown, and trim...
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img9} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Using stencils, sketch out decorations. Roll out fondant, trim into decorations, and attach to hat with piping gel...
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img10} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Decorate your hat as you choose. Use royal icing, fondant and sequin sprinkles.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img11} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Paint hat with your choice of edible colors...
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<nested_caption>My friends Joyce Salimeno-Gitlin and her late husband Mel were buzzing down Lake Louise Marie Road in Rock Hill, NY one day in 2018 when they noticed activity at the site of the old Dodge Inn restaurant. Mel—who had owned and operated restaurants and bars himself for years—was intrigued.</nested_caption>

“What do you think is going on over there?” he asked.

Always adventurous, Joyce and Mel decided to stop in and find out. And that’s when they met Luis Cerna.

As it turned out, Luis, his wife Annie Perez and son Brandon had been in the Rock Hill area for more than 20 years. As such, they’d driven past the boarded-up restaurant hundreds of times. And then Luis—who also owns Cerna’s Meat at the Hunt’s Point Meat Market in the Bronx—had a brainwave. Why not open a Tex-Mex restaurant in Rock Hill?

“I wanted to do Tex-Mex,” Luis explains, “because you can have delicious steaks on the menu.”

Excited and enthusiastic, the family purchased the building. Of course, having fallen into disuse for the better part of a decade, the structure needed work—a lot of work.

Rolling up their sleeves, they installed a kitchen made from stainless steel. They overhauled the dining rooms, adding a service bar and covering the walls in bright, beautiful murals. And they added some new stone and a waterfall to go along with the huge, iconic fireplace that remains from the Dodge Inn days.

They redid the main bar area as well. Adding stone to that back wall led to an important discovery.

“This new wall might be too heavy for the floor,” the mason told Luis one day. “You might want to go downstairs and check it out.”

Concerned, Luis went into the basement. While clearing debris, it became obvious to him that there had once been a fire down there. He’d have to add braces for sure, and might also have to redo much of the flooring.

And then—while digging more deeply into the rubble—he unearthed a tiny, doll-sized, straw sombrero, decorated with what looked like dried beans.

“How did this ever get down here?” Luis remembers thinking.

Later, he showed the artifact to Annie and Brandon. Everybody had a good laugh. They brought it home, Brandon tossed it into a drawer, and they all forgot about it.

Meanwhile, time marched on and work on the restaurant progressed. They began to start thinking about names.

“We got on the computer,” Luis explains, “and we compiled a list of 250 names. We narrowed that down to 200. Then, to150. We did like that all the way to 25.”

And that’s when Brandon came into the room carrying the tiny sombrero.

“Dad, do you want this for anything?”

Right then and there, Luis knew that they had their name: El Sombrero.

“It was just meant to be,” he says with a laugh.

It was shortly after that when Joyce and Mel made their surprise visit. While Luis picked Mel’s brain, they learned that Luis and Annie were close to opening up.

“You might like to go see what they’re doing there,” Joyce and Mel told me. “It looks beautiful, and it sounds like their food is going to be excellent.”

Of course, as it turns out, they were right.

The menu is based on recipes developed by Luis. They’re steeped in the Tex-Mex tradition. So, you’ll find unique tacos, enchiladas, fajitas, tostadas, guacamole and the like. You’ll also find New York strip steaks, grilled chicken, shrimp dishes, salmon, beef short ribs and the Sombrero Burger. There are a variety of salads, and the appetizers include calamari and chicken tenders. There are daily specials as well, which generally include an osso bucco that draws consistent praise. And, of course, there are the famous, best-selling Sombrero Samplers—for one, two or four—that feature spare ribs, short ribs, skirt steak, chicken, shrimp and spicy sausage—along with rice and beans, guacamole and pico de gallo.

And the drinks are excellent, too. You have a variety of wine and beer, plus there are daily drink specials. There is homemade sangria, margaritas, mojitos and my signature drink, the moquila—a mojito made with tequila.

The people are great. Luis, Annie and Brandon are usually on site, and guests are greeted at the door by Annie like they’re family. The décor is bright and tasteful—including a plastic cactus that sports the doll-sized sombrero—and the kitchen, bar, dining rooms and bathrooms are immaculate.

“The night doesn’t end until the kitchen is clean,” Luis explains. “I’m the inspector!”

Unlike some other places, El Sombrero came through the pandemic and managed to prosper. In addition to doing a brisk take-out business—which continues to this day—Annie and Luis made hundreds of “grab-and-go” meals for folks involved in health care.

And the pandemic, as bad as it was, showed Annie and Luis just how loyal their clientele is. I remember picking up an order at the end of April 2020, and Luis marveling that he’d made his monthly expenses. Since reopening in June of that year, El Sombrero has prospered. Luis, Annie, and Brandon are certainly grateful for that.

“The best part of this business is the customers,” Luis says today. “We welcome everyone like it’s their first time. We pay attention to service, atmosphere, food and price. We cook like it’s for our own family.”

That’s why the project this month is the Chocolate Sombrero Piece. We’ve been more-than-regular customers since El Sombrero opened, and I’ve seen how far Luis, Annie and Brandon are willing to go to make sure that the El Sombrero experience is always a quality one. With my food allergies—remember, I’m the cake artist who can’t eat cake—I’m always appreciative when dining establishments let me work the menu a little bit to ensure that I don’t get sick.

As always, have fun with your project. Experiment with different colors and different shapes. Make what you do your own. That’s the meaning of art.

And with Cinco de Mayo coming up soon, maybe take some time to journey out to El Sombrero. And ask for a moquila while you’re there!

<Youtube id="TEKgKHqE5FU" title="YouTube video player" client:load />
```

```---
<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Surprise candy pours out of the chocolate pumpkin.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

“Trick or treat!”

It’s Halloween time again, and after the “trick” of 2020, we can certainly hope that Halloween 2021 is a “treat.”

Of course, this Halloween is extra-special for me since I can currently be seen competing on the 11th season of Food Network’s “Halloween Wars” (Sundays at 9 p.m., EDT). Make sure you tune in—all of the teams on the show really stepped up and created some cake art that will blow your mind!

In keeping with the “treat” theme, this month’s project is a fun one: a hollowed-out chocolate pumpkin complete with a surprise! It’s a joyful variation of the classic piñata… and if there are little kids in your life—or fun-loving adults—they will be thrilled!

The key element is the pumpkin mold. These aren’t hard to find—check baker’s supply stores or search online. A little effort finding the right mold now will pay off in many surprise pumpkins in the future.

Enjoy the project and have a Happy Halloween!

<Youtube id="0W-QJin4HNI" title="YouTube video player" client:load />
```

make-this-crystal-amethyst-cake-topper.mdx:

```---
title: "Make this crystal amethyst cake topper"
date: 2021-01-27
draft: true
description: "I’m often asked, “Who’s your favorite artist?” “Mother Nature,” I always reply. I’m very much in tune with nature and the elements. A lot of my …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-this-crystal-amethyst-cake-topper,41546"
image: "@assets/blog/make-this-crystal-amethyst-cake-topper/20210127-090530-04fd-_kim-_Amethyst_cake_Topper.jpg"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/make-this-crystal-amethyst-cake-topper/20210127-090530-04fd-_kim-_Amethyst_cake_Topper.jpg';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/make-this-crystal-amethyst-cake-topper/20210127-091156-Amethyst_20supply_20list.jpg';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/make-this-crystal-amethyst-cake-topper/20210127-091156-Amethyst_201.jpg';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/make-this-crystal-amethyst-cake-topper/20210127-091146-Amethyst_202.jpg';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/make-this-crystal-amethyst-cake-topper/20210127-091147-Amethyst_203.jpg';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-this-crystal-amethyst-cake-topper/20210127-091149-Amethyst_204.jpg';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-this-crystal-amethyst-cake-topper/20210127-091150-Amethyst_205.jpg';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-this-crystal-amethyst-cake-topper/20210127-091151-Amethyst_206.jpg';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/make-this-crystal-amethyst-cake-topper/20210127-091153-Amethyst_207.jpg';
import img10 from '@assets/blog/make-this-crystal-amethyst-cake-topper/20210127-091154-Amethyst_208.jpg';
import img11 from '@assets/blog/make-this-crystal-amethyst-cake-topper/20210127-091155-Amethyst_209.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        According to Mycrystals.com, “The eye-catching amethyst crystals have a long history of meanings and properties, all of which are positive, as they were known to bring forth the purest aspirations of human kind.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

I’m often asked, “Who’s your favorite artist?”

“Mother Nature,” I always reply.

I’m very much in tune with nature and the elements. A lot of my artistic ideas are sparked by what I see in the natural world.

Stones, in particular, are fascinating to me. Beliefs about the spiritual properties of certain stones and minerals go back to the beginning of human history.

Amethyst is one of my favorites. I like crystals in general—I love the shapes and patterns and the way crystal catches light. An added benefit of amethyst has to do with its spiritual properties, which include healing and protection.

It also happens to be the birthstone for the month of February.

According to Mycrystals.com, “The eye-catching amethyst crystals have a long history of meanings and properties, all of which are positive, as they were known to bring forth the purest aspirations of humankind... It was also used in ancient China as a powerful tool to clear negative energy and drive away the hazards of everyday life.”

That’s why I thought this cake topper would be a perfect project for you to tackle this coming month.

Just a note of caution—the amethyst cake topper features the use of isomalt, which has to be heated to very high temperatures. If you’re not careful, you can burn yourself badly—as I was reminded a few weeks ago when I burned my own thumb building one of my pieces. Please take precautions and wear gloves.

Other than that, the project is user-friendly and results in something beautiful. As always, feel free to experiment with whatever shapes and colors express you!

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Materials needed.

<Youtube id="3-A3aDQ5drQ" title="YouTube video" client:load />

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using a silicone cup, heat clear Isomalt up in microwave at 15-second intervals until it bubbles (remember, Isomalt is very hot; gloves are recommended).

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

When the bubbles dissipate, pour clear Isomalt a third of the way into molds, then roll it around so it covers the inside.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Heat purple Isomalt at 15-second intervals until it bubbles. When the bubbles dissipate, pour up to about halfway into the mold.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Repeat with the white Isomalt. Pour almost to the top.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Repeat with the black Isomalt. Pour to the top. Let cool.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

When crystals are cold, remove them from the molds.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img9} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Now, reheat all four colors and set aside. Starting with the white Isomalt, pour a dot in the center of the cookie. Continue pouring rings around the white, alternating the colors to develop your Geo. Cover the entire cookie.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img10} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using your torch, burn out the excess bubbles, then set aside to cool.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img11} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using your clear Isomalt as glue, pour or dip your crystal pieces and attach to Geo cookie. Torch out the excess bubbles, then enjoy your Amethyst Geo Cake Topper!
```

```---
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093858-kim.JPG';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-094222-kim.JPG';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093842-Cute_20Scarecrow_201.JPG';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093843-Cute_20Scarecrow_202.JPG';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093843-Cute_20Scarecrow_203.JPG';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093844-Cute_20Scarecrow_204.JPG';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093845-Cute_20Scarecrow_205.JPG';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093846-Cute_20Scarecrow_206.jpg';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093847-Cute_20Scarecrow_207.JPG';
import img10 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093848-Cute_20Scarecrow_208.JPG';
import img11 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093848-Cute_20Scarecrow_209.JPG';
import img12 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093849-Cute_20Scarecrow_2010.JPG';
import img13 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093850-Cute_20Scarecrow_2011.JPG';
import img14 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093850-Cute_20Scarecrow_2012.JPG';
import img15 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093851-Cute_20Scarecrow_2013.JPG';
import img16 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093852-Cute_20Scarecrow_2014.JPG';
import img17 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093853-Cute_20Scarecrow_2015.JPG';
import img18 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093854-Cute_20Scarecrow_2016.JPG';
import img19 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093854-Cute_20Scarecrow_2017.JPG';
import img20 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093855-Cute_20Scarecrow_2018.JPG';
import img21 from '@assets/blog/make-this-cute-scarecrow-cake-topper-out-of-fondant/20200826-093855-Cute_20Scarecrow_2019.JPG';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

I have always loved fall. In our corner of the world, it is absolutely beautiful. When I see brightly colored leaves on mountain trees against a blue sky, I feel invigorated. I feel that Mother Nature does her best work when there is a slight chill in the air.

I have also always been interested in scarecrows, from the funny to the creepy. I’m puzzled, though, as to why I’ve never seen one that wasn’t surrounded by crows.

And, of course, crows are very interesting. They’re extremely intelligent. According to “Facts About Crows” by Alina Bradford, “They are known for their problem-solving skills and amazing communication skills. For example, when a crow encounters a mean human, it will teach other crows how to identify the human. In fact, research shows that crows don’t forget a face” (www.livescience.com).

Given these interests, I thought it would be fun to make a scarecrow out of fondant for this month’s tutorial. Having a cute scarecrow to put on top of a cake or some other confection just epitomizes fall to me.

It’s true that fondant can be a little bit difficult to work with at first. But once you master some of the tricks—which you’ll see in the tutorial—it can open up a world of creative fun for a budding cake artist.

Remember, too, that you can be as creative as you’d like to be in terms of shapes and colors for your scarecrow cake topper. What I’ve done here is *one* way, not *the* way. Always remember to express yourself in your artwork.

I hope you enjoy making this cute scarecrow as much as I did.

<Youtube id="EXWAsu0o7Tc" title="YouTube video" client:load />

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

With blue fondant, make a two-inch cone for the scarecrow’s body.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Roll out blue fondant about 4 ½-inches long and ¼-inch thick; cut in half for the legs.Using a large ball tool, open up the bottom part of the pant legs.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Pinch the other end and attach to the body using a little bit of water and a sculpting tool.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Make a one-inch circle out of yellow fondant; using scissors, cut all the way around to make the straw.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using a little bit of water and the ball tool, add the straw to the bottom half of the legs.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using red fondant, make the top half of the body (about 2-inches high). Add to lower body. Use a wooden skewer that sticks out about a quarter-inch at the top for head.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img9} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Roll red fondant thin. Using a wheel cutter and textured wheel, cut jacket to fit. Add to the upper body using a little water to attach.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img10} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Roll a thin strip of red fondant to make the collar for the jacket. Cut a V for the front and wrap around the neck area using a little water to attach.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img11} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using a small textured wheel, add stitches to the jacket up and down both sides and the front.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img12} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using a large ball tool, open up the bottom part of the arms. Make a 1-inch circle out of yellow fondant; using scissors, cut all the way around to make the straw.Using a little bit of water and the ball tool, add the straw to the bottom half of the arms.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img13} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Pinch the ends of the arms and add to body with a little water.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img14} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Roll a thin piece of yellow fondant about a quarter-inch wide and 3 ½-inches long for the bow tie. Fold in half; cut bottom edges at an angle. Using a little water, add to body, then make a little yellow ball for the middle of the bow.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img15} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Make patches by using differently colored fondant. Roll tiny squares and then texture to look like stitching; add to jacket with a little water.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img16} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using a fondant of your choice, roll a ball about 2-inches for the head.Using a small ball tool, make indentations where the eyes will be and start shaping face. Using a clay tool, open up the mouth area; add a little bit of black fondant to the mouth area using clay tool and a little water.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img17} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Make two small balls to fit in the eye area out of white fondant; using a little water, add to the face.Using a little bit of deep pink fondant, make a small oval nose and add to the face.Place head onto body.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img18} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using some yellow fondant, make a 3-inch circle. Using scissors, cut all the way around with small cuts for the straw hair.Place on the head using a little water.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img19} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using brown fondant, roll out a 4-inch circle and place on head for hat. Using clay tool, add texture around the head.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img20} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Add details using fondant for the eyes and dust clothing with blue powdered edible paint, and dust straw and hat with brown.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img21} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using some black and yellow fondant, make a small crow to sit on the top of the hat.
```


make-this-cute-teddy-bear-out-of-fondant.mdx:

```---
title: "Make this cute teddy bear out of fondant"
date: 2024-06-25
description: "As I write this, I’m anticipating June 14 in a big way. Not because June 14 is Flag Day, although that was always a big deal in my house growing up (more on this later). No, I’m …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags:
  [
    fondant,
    teddy-bear,
    cake-decorating,
    sculpting,
    patriotic,
    flag-day,
    july-4th,
    kids-crafts,
    sugar-art,
  ]
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-this-cute-teddy-bear-out-of-fondant,153017"
image: "@assets/blog/make-this-cute-teddy-bear-out-of-fondant/bear.jpg"
imageAlt: "A red, white and blue teddy bear made from fondant sitting on a wooden surface"
showFeaturedImage: false
---

import { Image } from "astro:assets";
import img1 from "@assets/blog/make-this-cute-teddy-bear-out-of-fondant/bear.jpg";

<figure class="blog-image">
  <Image
    src={img1}
    alt="A red, white and blue teddy bear made from fondant sitting on a cupcake on a wooden surface"
  />
  <figcaption>
    This fondant teddy bear is a fun kids' project. It marks Flag Day,
    Juneteenth and the Fourth of July. Grab your ingredients and make patriotic
    bears for the day!
  </figcaption>
</figure>

As I write this, I’m anticipating June 14 in a big way.

Not because June 14 is Flag Day, although that was always a big deal in my house growing up (more on this later). No, I’m looking forward to June 14 this year because I’ll be meeting my newest grandson, Dakota, for the first time.

Dakota will be the fourth son born to my daughter Kayliegh and my son-in-law Jason. June 14 is certainly a fitting time for Dakota to arrive. As I mentioned above, it’s Flag Day. My dad—Jack Simons—was a decorated Korean War veteran who was wounded in action. He was awarded a Combat Infantry Badge and a Purple Heart. He later started the Clarence Hoyt VFW Post No. 9217 in Liberty, NY, and served as VFW State Commander as well.

On Flag Day, we always made sure that we flew Old Glory correctly. That meant that it didn’t touch the ground. We also knew not to fly it upside down unless there was an emergency. We also refrained from carrying it flat, or folding things inside of it. We always stored it where it wouldn’t get dirty. And we knew better than to wear it as clothing.

So, as you can imagine, this time of year was a very busy one for us growing up, what with Memorial Day, Flag Day on the 14th, and July 4 waiting in the wings. My mom and dad seemingly lived at the VFW, which meant that I was there a lot, too, waiting tables and cleaning up.

The cool part of it was the generosity the VFW extended. If you attended the ceremony on any given Memorial Day, Flag Day, or July 4, you were welcome to partake of the food and soft drinks served at the VFW later on for free. I can tell you that I learned a great deal about what hospitality means through my experiences working these events. And I met a lot of great people who remain lifelong friends. I wouldn’t trade those experiences for anything.

Of course, when we weren’t at the VFW, we were at the Liberty Volunteer Firehouse (Ontario Hose Co. #3). My dad and my mom Carol were big into that scene, too. Plus, my dad made us join the Little Bighorn Marching Band, which meant Yours Truly marching in parades, schlepping my beloved glockenspiel. (Please feel free to read “beloved glockenspiel” with more than a grain of sarcasm.)

Dad’s been gone since 2006, so he never got to meet his great-grandsons. I know he would have loved them. And I know he would have loved my fabulous dog, Ozzy, as well. My mom certainly does.

So by the time you read this, Flag Day will have passed, Juneteenth will also be a memory, and Dakota will have arrived. But July 4 will be right around the corner.

That’s why this month’s project is this cute teddy bear made out of fondant. I chose to dress him up in patriotic colors in honor of Flag Day, Juneteenth and July 4. But he’s also sporting the red, white and blue in honor of my dad, who loved teddy bears and collected them. He even had one that sang “America, the Beautiful.” It remains at my mom’s house to this day.

<Youtube
  id="9jlqhYzggdM"
  title="Teddy Bear Out of Fondant video tutorial"
  client:load
/>

I specifically wanted this project to be easy for kids to do. As you’ll see, it’s a pretty simple sculpting job. And, of course, you don’t have to go with the red, white and blue motif if you don’t want to. As always, let your creativity run free and let your teddy bear be, well, your teddy bear.

If you follow the news, you know that—as this July 4 approaches—there is a lot to divide us. But we should never forget to think about and appreciate the things that unite us. The future generations of Americans—Dakota included—deserve no less.
```

```---

date: 2023-06-21

description: "Remember when we were kids and used to climb trees? This isn’t something you see often anymore. At least, I don’t. But back in the day around my neighborhood in Liberty, NY, …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-this-gingerbread-tree-of-life,102551"
image: "@assets/blog/make-this-gingerbread-tree-of-life/20230621-131607-25fd-_kim-_main.jpg"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/make-this-gingerbread-tree-of-life/20230621-131607-25fd-_kim-_main.jpg';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/make-this-gingerbread-tree-of-life/20230621-131802-Gingerbread_20Tree_20of_20Life_20Supply_20List.jpg';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/make-this-gingerbread-tree-of-life/20230621-131715-Gingerbread_20Tree_20of_20Life_201.jpg';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/make-this-gingerbread-tree-of-life/20230621-131719-Gingerbread_20Tree_20of_20Life_202.jpg';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/make-this-gingerbread-tree-of-life/20230621-131724-Gingerbread_20Tree_20of_20Life_203.jpg';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-this-gingerbread-tree-of-life/20230621-131729-Gingerbread_20Tree_20of_20Life_204.jpg';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-this-gingerbread-tree-of-life/20230621-131735-Gingerbread_20Tree_20of_20Life_205.jpg';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-this-gingerbread-tree-of-life/20230621-131739-Gingerbread_20Tree_20of_20Life_206.jpg';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/make-this-gingerbread-tree-of-life/20230621-131744-Gingerbread_20Tree_20of_20Life_207.jpg';
import img10 from '@assets/blog/make-this-gingerbread-tree-of-life/20230621-131749-Gingerbread_20Tree_20of_20Life_208.jpg';
import img11 from '@assets/blog/make-this-gingerbread-tree-of-life/20230621-131754-Gingerbread_20Tree_20of_20Life_209.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        The gingerbread Tree of Life
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Remember when we were kids and used to climb trees?

This isn’t something you see often anymore. At least, I don’t.

But back in the day around my neighborhood in Liberty, NY, there wasn’t a warm afternoon that went by without some time spent ascending tree branches hand-over-hand. If there was a tree, I tried to climb it.

And you know what? I was better than most of the dudes!

Of course, once I got up there, I savored the views as well as the overall experience. It’s hard to express what trees mean to me. Call me crazy, but I always felt as if they would talk to me. There’s something majestic about them, something that touches me, that I just can’t define.

High above the ground, supported by strong maple or chestnut branches, I was sure I was safe and was convinced I could see for a hundred miles. And if I sat still and kept quiet long enough, the birds would forget I was up there, so I could observe their behavior up close. I even sat with an owl one time, which was amazing.

To this day, I enjoy studying trees, photographing them and painting them. I find their varied textures to be endlessly fascinating. To me, trees are life.

This is not to say that every time I climbed a tree, I experienced Mother Nature in perfect bliss. You might remember the days before leash laws—most dogs then were allowed to roam around at will.

One time, a couple of big dogs—they were Dobermans, if I’m remembering correctly—got it into their heads that they needed to suddenly dash over to me and a group of friends. We didn’t know whether or not they were trying to meet us, or take our arms off at the shoulder—and we didn’t intend to find out.

Instead, we ripped into high gear, fanned out, and sprinted to the nearest trees, practically flying up the trunks to safety.

It felt like four days before those puppies got bored enough to walk away so we could climb back down.

As I’ve noted above, I appreciate trees for reasons other than just climbing. Plus, the symbolism attached to trees is powerful. They can represent endurance, immortality, strength and courage.

And we have family trees, which feature the branches of our ancestry, as I was reminded on a recent trip to Lew Beach, looking for the gravestone of my great grandparents, Ann and John Backman. (They owned the old Lew Beach Villa. Keep your eyes open for the rest of the story in the next Upper Delaware magazine supplement in the **River Reporter**.)

That’s why this month’s project is a gingerbread Tree of Life. Trees, to me, represent freedom. Their branches grow, reaching for the sun. Plus, the project is easy to do and makes for a great decoration.

As always, feel free to make my project yours. Experiment with different shapes, textures and styles. Make the tree of your own life.

And if you don’t climb a tree today, perhaps make a promise to take a minute and admire one.

<Youtube id="t1d1xeuNHWM" title="YouTube video player" client:load />
```

make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie.mdx:

```---
title: "Make this Halloween shaker cookie"
date: 2020-09-23
draft: true
description: "I’ve always loved Halloween. Not only does it happen during my favorite season—fall—but it represents much of what I love about Mother Nature. As a kid in Liberty, NY, I …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie,39546"
image: "@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094136-Halloween_Shaker_Cookie.JPG"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094136-Halloween_Shaker_Cookie.JPG';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094129-Halloween_20Shaker_20Cookie_20Supply_20List.JPG';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094113-Halloween_20Shaker_20Cookie_201.JPG';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094114-Halloween_20Shaker_20Cookie_202.JPG';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094115-Halloween_20Shaker_20Cookie_203.JPG';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094117-Halloween_20Shaker_20Cookie_204.JPG';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094118-Halloween_20Shaker_20Cookie_205.JPG';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094119-Halloween_20Shaker_20Cookie_206.JPG';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094119-Halloween_20Shaker_20Cookie_207.JPG';
import img10 from '@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094120-Halloween_20Shaker_20Cookie_208.JPG';
import img11 from '@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094121-Halloween_20Shaker_20Cookie_209.JPG';
import img12 from '@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094125-Halloween_20Shaker_20Cookie_2010.JPG';
import img13 from '@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094126-Halloween_20Shaker_20Cookie_2011.JPG';
import img14 from '@assets/blog/make-this-halloween-shaker-cookie/20200923-094127-Halloween_20Shaker_20Cookie_2012.JPG';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

I’ve always loved Halloween.

Not only does it happen during my favorite season—fall—but it represents much of what I love about Mother Nature.

As a kid in Liberty, NY, I really looked forward to Halloween. My neighborhood definitely got into it—decorations were the rule rather than the exception. I loved the idea of masks and costumes. I could be anybody I wanted to be! And it was great to see my friends doing the same thing, expressing themselves completely for maybe the only time during the year.

Of course, there was the candy. Yes, in some places, local health officials recommended having candy x-rayed, but in my neighborhood, we knew everybody, so that wasn’t a problem.

For me, though, candy was never the draw; half the time, I wouldn’t even go up to the door with my bag. I was just thrilled to be outside at night. I remember walking through piles of fallen leaves on Halloween. I can still hear the rustling, and I can still smell that sharp but comforting scent.

And the colors! Halloween features orange, black, purple and green; these are some of my favorites because they are so powerful.

To this day, I enjoy getting out on Halloween, seeing the giggling kids in their costumes. I’m proud to say my old neighborhood still does Halloween right.

Hence, this fun Halloween Shaker cookie. The skill level required to do this project is slightly advanced, but if you’re patient, the results are well worth it.

I do want to caution you a little bit about working with isomalt (which this cookie requires). It needs to be heated to very high temperatures, so you should take care not to get burned. I recommend wearing gloves, even though in the video I don’t—do as I say, not as I do!

But that’s really the only difficulty in doing this project. And the smiles you’ll see on the faces of children who get to shake their Halloween cookie will live in your memory forever.

<Youtube id="RO0fR4BjZ8Y" title="YouTube video" client:load />

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Materials needed.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Roll out sugar cookie dough about a quarter-inch thick.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using a poster board template and X-Acto blade, cut out designs. Make two opposite pieces.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Make the inner piece by cutting a border, about 3/4-inch all the way around.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using parchment paper, put on baking sheet and bake cookie as directed in your recipe. Let cool.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Sketch out your design on the top piece.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using Royal Icing, decorate your top cookie.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img9} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

On the opposite piece, use yellow Royal Icing to fill in to the edges.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img10} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Add border piece on top of back piece (to be sandwiched in the middle); set aside to let dry.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img11} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Add highlights and shadows by dusting with edible powder color.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img12} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

When cookie is totally dry and hardened, heat up your isomalt. Pour a thin layer of isomalt into the mouth, eyes, and nose areas of both cookies (wear gloves--isomalt is very hot!)...Let cool.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img13} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Take bottom cookie piece and add Halloween sprinkles.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img14} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using some more Royal Icing on the outer ridge of the bottom cookie, glue the top cookie. Let dry, and shake!
```


make-this-hector-s-inn-gummy-beer.mdx:

```---
title: "Make this Hector’s Inn gummy beer"
date: 2024-01-23
draft: true
description: "It’s late in July 1969 when farmer Max Yasgur, 49, agrees to allow the Aquarian Exposition—better known as the Woodstock Music and Art Fair—to happen on his property in Bethel, NY. …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-this-hectors-inn-gummy-beer,132505"
image: "@assets/blog/make-this-hector-s-inn-gummy-beer/20240123-074231-04fd-_Kim-_main.jpg"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/make-this-hector-s-inn-gummy-beer/20240123-074231-04fd-_Kim-_main.jpg';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/make-this-hector-s-inn-gummy-beer/20240123-073417-Gummy_20Beer_20Supplies.jpg';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/make-this-hector-s-inn-gummy-beer/20240123-073247-Gummy_20Beer_201.jpg';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/make-this-hector-s-inn-gummy-beer/20240123-073300-Gummy_20Beer_202.jpg';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/make-this-hector-s-inn-gummy-beer/20240123-073308-Gummy_20Beer_203.jpg';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-this-hector-s-inn-gummy-beer/20240123-073315-Gummy_20Beer_204.jpg';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-this-hector-s-inn-gummy-beer/20240123-073325-Gummy_20Beer_205.jpg';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-this-hector-s-inn-gummy-beer/20240123-073333-Gummy_20Beer_206.jpg';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/make-this-hector-s-inn-gummy-beer/20240123-073344-Gummy_20Beer_207.jpg';
import img10 from '@assets/blog/make-this-hector-s-inn-gummy-beer/20240123-073353-Gummy_20Beer_208_20_281_29.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Gummy beer
    </figcaption>
</figure>

It’s late in July 1969 when farmer Max Yasgur, 49, agrees to allow the Aquarian Exposition—better known as the Woodstock Music and Art Fair—to happen on his property in Bethel, NY.

Not long after, he makes his way about two miles up Route 17B from his Cochecton home to visit Hector’s Inn at 14 Dr. Duggan Rd. in Bethel.

“Listen,” he says to John “Jerry” Hector, the proprietor, “I’m letting them do that concert on my farm. They’re expecting fifty thousand people a day. You might want to stock up.”

Jerry—a lifelong Bethel resident who will turn 39 on August 30—knows exactly what “that concert” is—and that there is major controversy surrounding it. Almost immediately, there are calls to block the event and boycott Yasgur’s milk.

Not so fast, says the 90-member Town of Bethel Businessman’s Association. They back the three-day event “of peace and music” 100 percent, and even hope to make it an annual happening.

“This is a shot in the arm to the town economy,” an excited TBBA president Kenneth Van Loan crows, “and the greatest thing that ever happened to Sullivan County. And it just fell into our lap.”

Jerry Hector agrees with them. And he plans to take action.

Bold action.

But first, a little history…

Hector’s Inn initially opened in 1949, when Jerry’s parents—Howard and Elsie Hector—decided to convert their home into a bar. The Hectors were always keen on inviting folks to pull up a barstool and stay for a drink. They also gifted homegrown vegetables to local families in need.

When Howard died in the mid-1960s, it fell to Jerry and his wife Kay to carry on the traditions—which they were happy to do. They hosted clam bakes, steak bakes and Oktoberfests.

Plus, they treated their customers like family. Anyone who was broke didn’t leave Hector’s without a meal and a couple of bucks in their pocket. They also didn’t leave without a nickname, since Jerry is infamously unable to remember real ones.

In addition to running Hector’s, Jerry and Kay were into the stock car scene—Jerry raced in both White Lake, NY and Middletown, NY for a number of years. Having served in Korea, Jerry was also in the VFW. Plus, he wa a member of the Middletown Elks and served as an honorary member of the White Lake Fire Department.

And now—in 1969—he’s thinking about what Yasgur’s news about a three-day festival might mean for Hector’s Inn.

Rolling the dice, Jerry and Kay characteristically go all in, mortgaging Hector’s. They fill a tractor-trailer with $5 cases of Rheingold beer.

Well, those “fifty thousand” attendees per day turn out to be closer to an estimated four or five hundred thousand. And when Route 17B becomes a parking lot on or around August 15, all of that beer sells out. Fast.

But Kay and Jerry aren’t all about profit. When word comes that food is getting scarce at the rainy, mud-soaked concert, they make piles of sandwiches and run them over to grateful attendees.

So, it’s more than fitting that the first dove revealed on the Sullivan Catskills Dove Trail—which I am honored to have designed and painted—is the one proudly displayed outside of Hector’s Inn. (For more information on the trail, see: sullivancatskills.com/sullivan-catskills-dove-trail/).

Meanwhile, helping Jerry and Kay—amidst the whirlwind that is Woodstock 1969—is their daughter, Bonnie.

And Bonnie—along with her husband, Barry—is running Hector’s to this day.

The change of hands happens gradually. Sadly, Kay dies in 2010. A brokenhearted Jerry forges ahead until his own death on July 27, 2012 at the age of 81.

Once Bonnie and Barry take over, they determine to keep the home-away-from-home feel, but also add some polish—including a state-of-the-art pavilion on the grounds outside for concerts and open mic events.

Plus, there is no holiday that Bonnie and Barry don’t celebrate by throwing a party, where they generously offer a variety of food—usually at no charge.

Bonnie even hosts a monthly birthday party for steady patrons that features food and a cake.

This in addition to the car shows, the Sip-and-Paints (which I’m honored to teach), the charitable events (e.g., for St. Jude’s Children’s Hospital), the annual Hamburger Day (where delicious burgers are grilled to perfection outside), the Mary Kay parties, the pool league, the O.B. meetings…

The list is truly endless.

Oh, and a game of pool still costs only 75 cents. It’s no wonder that Hector’s Inn was voted “Best Neighborhood Bar” by the readers of this paper in 2019, 2020 and 2022.

This is why my project this month is a glass of Hector’s Inn Gummy Beer. As I mentioned last month, the popularity of the TV show “Is It Cake?” is at least partly due to the fact that people love cutting into something they have no idea is cake—until they do.

Well, the same holds true for this gelatinous confection. People will be amazed that what looks like beer tastes like—in this case, anyway—an orange.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Sprinkle gelatin over the water. Use a spoon to push the gelatin down into the water.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Add 6 drops of flavoring into the gelatin mixture and stir.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Add color to the gelatin mixture.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Stir and let set about 5 minutes.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Microwave for 30-second increments, stirring in between until mixture is clear and there are no visible granules. Do NOT overheat. Pour mixture into large paper cup about 3/4 of the way. Pour leftover gelatin in a small bowl.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Melt leftover gelatin. Dissolve in microwave in 30 second increments. Use immersion blender or whisk to make bubbles in gelatin.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img9} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Spoon foam into cup with the chilled gelatin mixture and let set about 15 minutes. Remove the gummy beer from the cup—use an X-Acto blade to cut the cup and peel it off the gelatin beer.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img10} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Reheat the leftover gelatin and add the foam to the top. Let some drip down the sides.
    </figcaption>
</figure>


As always with these projects, I encourage you to be creative. Feel free to experiment with any flavors you may like. Gummy Beer can serve as a decorative item, a unique treat or even a conversation piece.

Oh, and if you happen to find yourself in Bethel NY—and are of age—stop by Hector’s Inn, pull up a barstool, and have a friendly drink.
```


make-this-holiday-gnome-out-of-fondant.mdx:

```---
title: "Make this holiday gnome out of fondant"
date: 2022-12-22
draft: true
description: "My mother was born on Christmas Day, which is why her name is Carol. But to hear her tell it, that wasn’t necessarily a great thing—at least for her. She was the youngest of four in a …"
categories: [Kim's Kitchen]
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-this-holiday-gnome-out-of-fondant,72476"
image: "@assets/blog/make-this-holiday-gnome-out-of-fondant/20221222-083320-51fd-_Kim-_main.jpg"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/make-this-holiday-gnome-out-of-fondant/20221222-083320-51fd-_Kim-_main.jpg';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/make-this-holiday-gnome-out-of-fondant/20221222-083325-HOLIDAY_20GNOME_20SUPPLY_20LIST.jpg';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/make-this-holiday-gnome-out-of-fondant/20221222-083336-HOLIDAY_20GNOME_201.jpg';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/make-this-holiday-gnome-out-of-fondant/20221222-083338-HOLIDAY_20GNOME_202.jpg';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/make-this-holiday-gnome-out-of-fondant/20221222-083339-HOLIDAY_20GNOME_203.jpg';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-this-holiday-gnome-out-of-fondant/20221222-083333-HOLIDAY_20GNOME_204.jpg';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-this-holiday-gnome-out-of-fondant/20221222-083327-HOLIDAY_20GNOME_206.jpg';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-this-holiday-gnome-out-of-fondant/20221222-083329-HOLIDAY_20GNOME_207.jpg';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/make-this-holiday-gnome-out-of-fondant/20221222-083331-HOLIDAY_20GNOME_208.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        The completed holiday gnome.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

My mother was born on Christmas Day, which is why her name is Carol. But to hear her tell it, that wasn’t necessarily a great thing—at least for her.

She was the youngest of four in a German/Puerto Rican family, and the only girl. But it was as if only Christmas came on December 25. My mom’s birthday wasn’t really celebrated until she got married in 1958.

My father was Jewish, but he loved Christmas. He also insisted that my mom was to be honored on the day she was born. He decreed that presents for her birthday should be wrapped in birthday paper, not Christmas wrap, and that she should choose a special meal for Christmas Eve—which, to this day, is Chinese food.

“He’d also hang five dollars in an envelope on the tree for me,” she remembers today. (Adjusted for inflation, that would be worth slightly less than $50 as of this writing.)

We would also put up the tree on Christmas Eve, which my dad loved, even though they would battle every year about how, exactly, to handle the tinsel.

Mom wanted to lay it on, while Dad wanted all of us to throw it. Dad usually won—at least temporarily.

“I’d always redo it when he wasn’t looking,” my mother says now.

Among our most important decorations were two cloth elves, kind of like the Elf on the Shelf ornaments of today. These two characters were the objects of constant mischief. You never knew when someone was going to reposition them, hide them, or put strange objects in their hands. This tradition goes on to this day in my mother’s house.

Which is why this month’s project is a cute holiday gnome made out of fondant. It’s in honor of one of my mom’s favorite birthday traditions, plus it obviously has a connection to the holidays. Apparently an alchemist and philosopher called Paracelsus came up with the concept of gnomes in the early 1500s during the German Renaissance. In Paracelsus’ view, gnomes were small people and “earth-dwellers.”

The Christmas connection comes from Scandinavia, where gnomes—like Santa Claus here—bring gifts to good children on Christmas Eve. Gnomes and elves weren’t considered to be related until the works of J.R.R. Tolkien, where gnomes became a type of elf. Go figure.

Whatever the case may be, there’s no denying that this will be a fun project. As always, feel free to be creative and shape/color your holiday gnome whichever way you would like.

Oh, and don’t forget to throw some tinsel at the tree!

<Youtube id="glBHE2ZJECQ" title="Make This Adorable Holiday Gnome" client:load />

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Shape the body, head and hat out of Rice Krispie treats.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img4} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Roll out green fondant, cut with utility knife, and cover the body.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Roll some fondant (your choice of color) into a ball and add to the face as a nose.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Roll out and cut some more green fondant. Cover the hat.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Make arms out of fondant. Attach to body. Make hands out of fondant. Attach to arms.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Roll out strips of fondant for beard. Attach with cake tool.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img9} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
        Roll out balls of fondant for boots and attach to body.
    </figcaption>
</figure>

- Shape the body, head and hat out of Rice Krispie treats.
- Roll out green fondant, cut with utility knife, and cover the body.
- Roll some fondant (your choice of color) into a ball and add to the face as a nose.
- Roll out and cut some more green fondant. Cover the hat.
- Make arms out of fondant. Attach to body.
- Make hands out of fondant. Attach to arms.
- Roll out strips of fondant for beard. Attach with cake tool.
- Roll out balls of fondant for boots. Attach to body.
```

```---
tags: []
author: kim-simons
url: "https://www.riverreporter.com/stories/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant,38027"
image: "@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200625-080744-26fd-_kim_s_eagle.jpg"
showFeaturedImage: true
---

import { Image } from 'astro:assets';
import img1 from '@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200625-080744-26fd-_kim_s_eagle.jpg';
import img2 from '@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200628-145221-Supplies_20Needed.jpg';
import img3 from '@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200628-144758-1.jpg';
import img4 from '@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200628-144759-2.jpg';
import img5 from '@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200628-144800-3.jpg';
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200628-144801-4.jpg';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200628-144802-5.jpg';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200628-144803-6.jpg';
import img9 from '@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200628-144804-7.jpg';
import img10 from '@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200628-144804-8.jpg';
import img11 from '@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200628-144805-9.jpg';
import img12 from '@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200628-144806-10.jpg';
import img13 from '@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200628-144807-11.jpg';
import img14 from '@assets/blog/make-this-liberty-eagle-out-of-fondant/20200628-144808-12.jpg';

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img1} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

I have always loved birds—raptors, in particular. When I see a hawk in flight, I know I’m going to have a good day.

I have also always been proud of where I come from—Liberty, NY. My high school graduating class is still very tight; we’ll get together for a big reunion as soon as COVID-19 allows.

Growing up, I spent a great deal of time at the Clarence Hoyt VFW Post. My father, Jack Simons, was a Liberty High School graduate and a wounded Korean War veteran who eventually became the VFW State Commander. He and my mother, Carol, instilled a sense of community volunteerism in me as well as a desire to do some good in the world... which I hope I’m doing through my artwork.

As the United States approaches its 244th birthday, it’s no secret that we’re facing tough times. But thinking about our local bald eagles might provide us with some insight and maybe even some hope.

According to the website “American Bald Eagle Information”, “The bald eagle was chosen June 20, 1782 as the emblem of the United States of America because of its long life, great strength and majestic looks, and also because it was then believed to exist only on this continent” ([www.baldeagleinfo.com](http://www.baldeagleinfo.com)). True to the eventual American character, not everyone was on board. Benjamin Franklin, for example, thought the bald eagle was a terrible choice. “He does not get his living honestly,” Franklin wrote. “You may have seen him perched on some dead tree, where, too lazy to fish for himself, he watches the labor of the fishing-hawk, and when that diligent bird has at length taken a fish, and is bearing it to its nest for the support of his mate and young ones, the bald eagle pursues him and takes it from him” ([www.baldeagleinfo.com](http://www.baldeagleinfo.com)). Franklin’s choice? The turkey.

For me, the bald eagle soaring over places like the Hawk’s Nest is majestic; I feel that they are pure spirits of strength and courage. We might also consider how farsighted and intelligent they are—qualities we need to hang on to today.

For these reasons, I chose to name this piece the Liberty Eagle. I hope you have fun making it!

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img2} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

<Youtube id="B_6hsEhTIBc" title="YouTube video" client:load />

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img3} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Cover cake, cookie... extra with white fondant

    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Roll out the field with navy blue fondant; it will be about 5” x 5”...attach to the top lefthand side of the piece

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img5} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Roll the stripes of red fondant; you will need three 1 1/4-inch thick x 9” long. Using a little water, attach, alternating with the white background.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img6} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Roll white fondant for the stars; you will need about seven 1/2” stars. Using a little water, attach alternating the rows.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img7} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Roll white fondant and cut out the head of the eagle about 6” x 6” using an X-Acto blade. Use a little water; attach in the middle of the piece.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img8} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Roll a 1-inch ball of yellow for the eye; using a little water, add on.

Roll yellow fondant and cut out the beak of the eagle about 2” x 4 using an X-Acto blade. Use a little water to attach.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img10} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Add texture to the head by rolling strips and attach, using a little water to show the flowing features.

<figure class="blog-image">
    <Image src={img11} alt="" />
    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using a clay tool, blend and add more texture to the head and beak.

<figure class="blog-image">

    <figcaption>
    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using flack fondant, roll a very small ball and flatten; add a little water to the center of the eye and attach.

Using edible colors, add shadow and highlights to the head, the beak, and around the eye.

    </figcaption>
</figure>

Using a very teeny amount of white fondant, add highlights and dust with edible paint.
```

```---
import img6 from '@assets/blog/make-this-mountain-view-cookie/20211124-133026-3_20_282_29.jpg';
import img7 from '@assets/blog/make-this-mountain-view-cookie/20211124-133027-4_20_282_29.jpg';
import img8 from '@assets/blog/make-this-mountain-view-cookie/20211124-133029-5_20_282_29.jpg';
```
